(X_{u})_{u\in U}
of finite sets that can be regarded as
a two-move sequential game (with no outcome) between player
\oplus
and
\with
:
first
\oplus
plays some
u\in U
and then
\with
plays some
x\in X_{u}
.
One can then consider
simulation games
between
(U,(X_{u})_{u})
(the “left hand-side”) and
(V,(Y_{v})_{v})
(the “right hand-side”)
proceeding as follows:

•

first,
\with
plays some
u\in U
on the left

•

then,
\oplus
plays some
v\in V
on the right

•

\with
answers with some
y\in Y_{v}
on the right

•

finally
\oplus
answers with
x\in X_{u}
on the left.

\begin{array}[]{l| l @{~}c@{~} ll l @{~}c@{~} @{~}c}&U\hfil\penalty\ &,\hfil\penalty\ &(X_{u})_{u}&\to&V,\hfil\penalty\ &(Y_{v})_{v}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
\hline\cr\with&u\hfil\penalty\ &\hfil\penalty\ &&&\hfil\penalty\ &\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
\oplus&\hfil\penalty\ &\hfil\penalty\ &&&v\hfil\penalty\ \\
\with&\hfil\penalty\ &\hfil\penalty\ &&&\hfil\penalty\ &y\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
\oplus&\hfil\penalty\ &\hfil\penalty\ &x\\
\end{array}

Morphisms in
\mathbbl{1}_{\oplus\with}
are
\oplus
-strategies in such games. Identities
correspond to copycat strategies and composition is (a simple version) of an usual scheme in game semantics.
As for
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
, one may consider that once this simulation game is played,
\oplus
needs to provide a datum in some
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{x},C_{y}\right)
which depends on the outcome of the game.

We write
\iota_{\oplus\with}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
for the (full and faithful) embedding
sending an object
C
to the one-element family
\bigoplus_{1}C
.
As the coproduct completion preserves limits, it means that
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
always boasts both
binary cartesian products and coproducts. Concretely, products are computed using the distributivity
of products over coproducts

\bigwith_{i\in I}\bigoplus_{j\in J_{i}}A_{j}\quad\cong\quad\bigoplus_{f\in\prod_{i\in I}J_{i}}\bigwith_{i\in I}A_{f(i)}

If
\mathcal{C}
has a symmetric monoidal structure
(\otimes,\mathbf{I})
, the lifting is computed
in
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
as

\bigotimes_{i\in I}\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{u\in U_{i}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigwith_{x\in X_{u}}\penalty\ \penalty\ C_{x}\qquad=\qquad\bigoplus_{f\in\prod\limits_{i\in I}U_{i}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigwith_{g\in\prod\limits_{i\in I}X_{i,f(i)}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigotimes_{i\in I}\penalty\ \penalty\ C_{g(i)}

3.6.1.
The monoidal closure theorem

Recall from the introduction that a result of central importance in this paper
is the fact that the category
(\mathcal{SR}_{\with})_{\oplus}
is symmetric monoidal
closed (Theorem
3
). This will be a consequence of the
general property below.

Theorem 47
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbf{I})
be a symmetric monoidal category. Assume that its
coproduct completion
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}
admits internal homsets

\iota_{\oplus}(A)\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(B)
for every
A,B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.
Then its Dialectica-like completion
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
is monoidal closed.

Indeed, since Lemma
36
gives us precisely the assumption on
internal homsets in the above statement for
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{SR}
, we immediately get:

Corollary 48

(equivalent to Theorem
3
)
.

\mathcal{SR}_{\oplus\with}
is monoidal closed.

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(A\otimes B,C\right)\\
\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\kern 0em}c}&=&{\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\left[\bigoplus_{w\in W}\bigwith_{z\in Z_{w}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\right]\otimes\left[\bigoplus_{u\in U}\bigwith_{x\in X_{u}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{x})\right]\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y})\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt\\
&=&{\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigoplus_{(w,u)\in W\times U}\bigwith_{(z,x)\in Z_{w}\times X_{u}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z}\otimes B_{x})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y})\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt\\
&\cong&{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\sum_{z\in Z_{w}}\sum_{x\in X_{u}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\otimes\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{x})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y})\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt\\
&\cong&{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\sum_{z\in Z_{w}}\sum_{x\in X_{u}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y}))\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt\\
&\cong&{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\sum_{z\in Z_{w}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{x\in X_{u}}\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y}))\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt&\text{\raisebox{20.00003pt}{$(\dagger)$}}\\
&\cong&{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigwith_{z\in Z_{w}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{x\in X_{u}}\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y}))\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt&\text{\raisebox{20.00003pt}{$(\heartsuit)$}}\\
&\cong&{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigwith_{z\in Z_{w}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{u}}\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y}))\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt\\
&\cong&{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{u\in U}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigwith_{z\in Z_{w}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{u}}\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus\with}(C_{y}))\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt&\text{\raisebox{20.00003pt}{$(\dagger)$}}\\
&\cong&{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigwith_{z\in Z_{w}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{z})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;B\multimap C\right)}\hfil\kern 0.0pt\\
&\cong&\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(A,B\multimap C\right)\hfil\kern 0.0pt\\
\end{array}\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 8.
Monoidal closure of
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
(Theorem
47
).

Let us now sketch how the proof of Theorem
47
goes.
To this end, assume that we have

A=\bigoplus_{u\in U}\bigwith_{x\in X_{u}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{x})
and

B=\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{y})
be objects of
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}

and assume that we have internal homsets
\iota_{\oplus\with}(A_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus\with}(B_{y})

for every
(u,x)\in\sum_{u\in U}X_{u}
and
(v,y)\in\sum_{v\in V}Y_{v}
.
Let

\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}:\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}\to\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}\quad\text{such that}\quad\iota_{\oplus\with}=\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}\circ\iota_{\oplus}

be the full and faithful embedding be obtained by applying the universal
property of
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}
to the coproduct-preserving functor

\iota_{\oplus\with}
.
The linear arrow
A\multimap B
can then be defined as follows

A\multimap B\quad=\quad\bigwith_{u\in U}\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{u}}\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus}(A_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(B_{y}))

It is clear that this is meant to mimick the definition of
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(-,-\right)

by substituting external hom, products and coproducts with internal ones, as is customary with
Dialectica. To show that
B\multimap-
is right adjoint to
-\otimes B
, one may directly
establish that we have an isomorphism of homsets

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(A\otimes B,C\right)\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(A,B\multimap C\right)

natural in
A
using the recipe of Figure
8
.
Unmarked steps follow from definitions or standard abstract nonsense (characterization of
internal (co)products in terms of external ones).
The two
(\dagger)
steps involve instances of the isomorphism

\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigwith_{x\in X}\iota_{\oplus\with}(Z_{x})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;D_{i}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \cong\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigwith_{x\in X}\iota_{\oplus\with}(Z_{x})\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{i\in I}D_{i}\right)

which holds for any family
(Z_{x})_{x\in X}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
and family
(D_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
.
This is a formal computation corresponding to the tail end of Proposition
28
,
which applies because of the equivalence
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}\cong\left(\mathcal{C}_{\with}\right)_{\oplus}
.

The most subtle step, labelled with
(\heartsuit)
, necessitates a more detailed computation that relies
crucially on the fact that
\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus}(A_{x})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(B_{y}))
is

\with
-free. This can be regarded as a generic isomorphism for arbitrary families
of objects
(E_{u})_{u\in U}
and
(Z_{x})_{x\in X}
of
\mathcal{C}

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigwith_{x\in X}\iota_{\oplus\with}(Z_{x}),\bigoplus_{u\in U}\iota_{\oplus\with}(E_{u})\right)\quad\cong\quad\sum_{x\in X}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(Z_{x},\bigoplus_{u\in U}\iota_{\oplus\with}(E_{u})\right)

which is easy to compute explicitly.

In addition to checking that there is a chain of isomorphisms as per
Figure
8
, it should also be checked that they are natural
in
A
. This requires identifying precisely what presheaves over
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}

are involved at every step and arguing that the maps are indeed natural.
This is not necessarily so straightforward, so we offer a more detailed proof of
Theorem
47
in Appendix
D
.

3.7.
Proof of the main result on strings

We can now give the proof of Theorem
1
, which can be summarized as the equality

\text{$\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}$-definable}\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{$\mathfrak{SR}$-SSTs}

thanks to Fact
17
. We start with the consequences of
our syntactic analysis

\text{$\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}$-definable}\;\underset{\overset{\uparrow}{\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:lamlsst}}}}{=}\;\text{single-state $\mathfrak{L}$-SSTs}\;\underset{\overset{\uparrow}{\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:coprod-whatever}}}}{=}\;\text{$\mathfrak{L}$-SSTs}

reducing our goal to

\text{$\mathfrak{L}$-SSTs}\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{$\mathfrak{SR}$-SSTs}

For the above equality, the right-to-left inclusion is simpler than its
converse: the existence of a morphism of streaming settings from
\mathfrak{SR}
to

\mathfrak{L}
(Lemma
26
) entails that
\mathfrak{L}
-SSTs subsume

\mathfrak{SR}
-SSTs (by Lemma
14
). (Were we only interested only proving
that regular functions are
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-definable, our setting would be a complete
overkill.)

On the other hand, the other direction mobilizes almost the whole development.
First, our semantic evaluation argument combines Lemma
27

with Corollary
48
to get

\text{$\mathfrak{L}$-SSTs}\penalty\ \penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{$\mathfrak{SR}_{\oplus\with}$-SSTs}

We then finish proving Theorem
1
with automata-theoretic
considerations:

\text{$\mathfrak{SR}_{\oplus\with}$-SSTs}\;\underset{\overset{\uparrow}{\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:sdmSST-oplus}}}}{=}\;\text{sdm-$\mathfrak{SR}_{\with}$-SSTs}\;\underset{\overset{\uparrow}{\text{Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:with-reg-conservativity}}}}{=}\;\text{sdm-$\mathfrak{SR}$-SSTs}

\text{sdm-$\mathfrak{SR}$-SSTs}\;\underset{\overset{\uparrow}{\text{Corollary\penalty\ \ref{cor:oplus-sst-reg-conservative}}}}{=}\;\text{$\mathfrak{SR}_{\oplus}$-SSTs}\;\underset{\overset{\uparrow}{\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:coprod-whatever}}}}{=}\;\text{$\mathfrak{SR}$-SSTs}

4.
Some transducer-theoretic applications of
\mathfrak{C}
-SSTs and internal homsets

This section is devoted to showing that the notion of monoidal closure can be used
to give a satisfying self-contained description of two important transformations of
usual streaming string transducers, both of which are not entirely straightforward:
the composition of two copyless SSTs and the uniformization of non-deterministic
copyless SSTs.

We take advantage of our abstract setting to give proofs for categorical generalizations of
those two statements, similarly in spirit to the generalized minimization argument
found in
[
CP20
]
. The specialized theorem then follows easily from
previous theorems in our developments having to do with monoidal closure, especially
Theorem
47
and Lemma
36
.

The ideas behind our arguments are not new; our main goal is to vindicate the view
that the notion of internal homset is the key to showing those results, even if it does
not appear explicitly in previous arguments.

4.1.
On closure under precomposition by regular functions

First, let us recall right off the bat that the closure under composition
of functions definable by copyless streaming string transducers follows
from our main result on strings (Theorem
1
) together with basic considerations
on typed
\lambda
-calculi (see e.g.
[
NP20
, Lemma 2.8]
that applies

mutatis mutandis
to the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus) that entail the closure under
composition of
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-definable functions. However, note that Theorem
1

relies on the syntactic analysis of Lemma
25
, which is arguably a non-trivial
result about the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus (as demonstrated by the size of
Appendices
B
and
C
, which are
both necessary to prove it).

The argument we give in this section circumvents that difficulty and does not
appeal to Theorem
1
nor to Lemma
25
.
That said, it still shares some (non-syntactic) ingredients with our proof of
Theorem
1
, namely:

•

the monoidal closure and quasi-affineness (see below) of
\mathcal{SR}_{\oplus\with}
;

•

the fact that
\mathfrak{SR}_{\oplus\with}
-SSTs are no more expressive than
\mathfrak{SR}
-SSTs.

These results still require substantial developments – indeed, this composition
property is quite non-trivial as mentioned in the introduction – but bypass the need of mentioning
the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus.

Beyond this simplification, the main advantage of our approach here is that we
get a more general theorem, that applies to many streaming settings; in
particular, the final output does not have to be a string.
Without further ado, let us state it.

Theorem 49
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a string streaming setting with output set
X
. Suppose that the
underlying category
\mathcal{C}
is
symmetric monoidal closed
and
quasi-affine
.
Furthermore, let us assume that
\text{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\Bot$}}}}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is equal to the monoidal unit

\mathbf{I}
.

Then for any
f:\Gamma^{*}\to X
computed by some
\mathfrak{C}
-SST, and any regular

g:\Sigma^{*}\to\Gamma^{*}
, the function
f\circ g:\Sigma^{*}\to X
is
computed by some (stateful)
\mathfrak{C}
-SST.
In other words, the class of functions defined by

\mathfrak{C}
-SSTs is
closed under precomposition by regular functions
.

Before proving the above theorem, let us check that it entails known
preservation and composition properties.

Corollary 50
.

Let
L\subseteq\Gamma^{*}
be a regular language and
g:\Sigma^{*}\to\Gamma^{*}
be a regular function. Then the language
g^{-1}(L)\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
is regular.

Proof 4.1
.

That
L
is regular is equivalent to its indicator function
\chi_{L}:\Gamma^{*}\to\{0,1\}
being computed by some single-state
\mathfrak{Finset}_{2}
-SST,
see Example
3.1
. The underlying category of finite sets is

cartesian closed
, and the monoidal structure given by a cartesian
product is automatically symmetric and affine. According to
Theorem
49
,
\chi_{L}\circ g
can therefore be computed
by some
\mathfrak{Finset}_{2}
-SST. Observing that the category of finite sets
has coproducts, and applying Lemma
34
, we even have a

single-state

\mathfrak{Finset}_{2}
-SST for
\chi_{L}\circ g
. Finally,
the latter is none other than the indicator function of
g^{-1}(L)
.

Corollary 51
.

Let
f:\Gamma^{*}\to\Delta^{*}
and
g:\Sigma^{*}\to\Gamma^{*}
be regular
functions. Then
f\circ g
is also a regular function.

Proof 4.2
.

This is just the application of Theorem
49
to

\mathfrak{SR}_{\oplus\with}
-SSTs – indeed, we saw at the very end of

Section

3
that the functions computed by
\mathfrak{SR}_{\oplus\with}
-SSTs are
exactly the regular functions. By Theorem
3
/
Corollary
48
, the underlying category

\mathcal{SR}_{\oplus\with}
is symmetric monoidal closed. Finally,
\mathcal{SR}_{\oplus\with}

is quasi-affine since it has all cartesian products by construction.

We now come to the proof of this generalized preservation theorem.

Proof 4.3

(Proof of Theorem
49
)
.

We give below a proof assuming that
f
is defined by some
single-state

\mathfrak{C}
-SST (but beware: the
\mathfrak{C}
-SST computing
f\circ g
will still be
stateful!). The general case can be applied by considering the streaming
setting
\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus\mathrm{const}}
-SST, as was briefly mentioned in
Remark
35
, and using the fact that single-state

\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus\mathrm{const}}
-SSTs, stateful

\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus\mathrm{const}}
-SSTs and stateful
\mathfrak{C}
-SSTs are equally
expressive. We leave it to the reader to check that the symmetric monoidal
structure and the cartesian products in
\mathcal{C}
can be lifted to

\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\mathrm{const}}
, making this generalization possible.

Therefore, we may assume without loss of generality that
f
is computed by a single-state
\mathfrak{C}
-SST

T_{f}=(\{\bullet\},A_{f},\delta_{f},i_{f},o_{f})
where
A_{f}
is an object of
\mathcal{C}
and

\delta_{f}:\Gamma\to\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A_{f},A_{f}\right)\qquad i_{f}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\text{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\Bot$}}}},A_{f}\right)\qquad o_{f}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A_{f},\Bot\right)

Let
T_{g}=(Q,q_{0},R_{g},\delta_{g},i_{g},o_{g})
be an usual copyless SST (i.e., a
\mathcal{SR}
-SST)
computing the regular function
g
, where
Q
and
R_{g}
are finite sets and

q_{0}\in Q\qquad\delta_{g}:\Sigma\times Q\to Q\times[R_{g}\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R_{g}]

i_{g}\in[\varnothing\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R_{g}]\qquad o_{g}:Q\to[R_{g}\to_{\mathcal{SR}}\{\bullet\}]

We will write
A=A_{f}
and
R=R_{g}
for short.

We want to build from this data a
\mathfrak{C}
-SST
T
defining
f\circ g
. Since

\mathcal{C}
is quasi-affine and symmetric monoidal closed, we can apply
Corollary
22
to the object
A\multimap A
and to a family of
morphisms
(\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(c))_{c\in\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A\multimap A,\;A\multimap A\right)^{\Gamma}
that will be defined later. This gives us a functor

F_{\delta_{f}}:\mathcal{SR}\to\mathcal{C}
, enjoying various properties that will be
progressively recalled, which is at the heart of our construction.

The set of states of our new
\mathfrak{C}
-SST
T
is
Q
, with initial state
q_{0}
,
and its memory object is
F_{\delta_{f}}(R)
. The initialization morphism is
defined as
i=F_{\delta_{f}}(i_{g})\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\mathbf{I},F_{\delta_{f}}(R)\right)
– we
use the assumption
\text{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\Bot$}}}}=\mathbf{I}
, and the fact that

F_{\delta_{f}}(\varnothing)=\mathbf{I}
(by Corollary
22
). The
transition function is

\begin{array}[]{c@{}c@{}c@{}c@{}c@{}c@{}c@{}c}\delta:&\quad\Sigma&\;\times&\;Q&\quad\longrightarrow&Q&\times&\quad\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(F_{\delta_{f}}(R),F_{\delta_{f}}(R)\right)\\
\\
&\quad c&\;,&q&\quad\mapsto&\quad\pi_{1}(\delta_{g}(c,q))&,&F_{\delta_{f}}(\pi_{2}(\delta_{g}(c,q)))\end{array}

Finally, using
j_{f}:F_{\delta_{f}}(\{\bullet\})\to A
to be defined later,
we take as our new output function

o:q\in Q\mapsto o_{f}\circ j_{f}\circ F_{\delta_{f}}(o_{g}(q))\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(F_{\delta_{f}}(R),\Bot\right)

Let us now sketch the verification that this defines the intended function
f\circ g:\Sigma^{*}\to X
. In the process, we will fill the missing
definitions to make everything work out.

Let
w=w_{1}\ldots w_{n}\in\Sigma^{*}
be an input string. The sequence

q_{0},\ldots,q_{n}\in Q
of states visited by both
T_{g}
and
T
when fed this
input is obtained by
q_{i+1}=\pi_{1}(\delta_{g}(w_{i},q_{i}))
from the initial

q_{0}
. By definition of the output of
T_{g}
, we have:

\widehat{g(w)}\quad=\quad o_{g}(q_{n})\circ\pi_{2}(\delta_{g}(w_{n},q_{n-1}))\circ\dots\circ\pi_{2}(\delta_{g}(w_{1},q_{0}))\circ i_{g}

where
\widehat{g(w)}\in[\varnothing\to_{\mathcal{SR}(\Gamma)}\{\bullet\}]

corresponds to
g(w)\in\Gamma^{*}
(cf. Theorem
20
)
and the ‘
\circ
’ denotes a composition of register transitions (i.e. of

\mathcal{SR}
-morphisms). Similarly, the output
T(w)
of the
\mathfrak{C}
-SST
T
that we
built on the input
w
is defined as

T(w)\quad=\quad{\llparenthesis o(q_{n})\circ F_{\delta_{f}}(\pi_{2}(\delta_{g}(w_{n},q_{n-1})))\circ\dots\circ F_{\delta_{f}}(\pi_{2}(\delta_{g}(w_{1},q_{0})))\circ i\rrparenthesis}

which, by unfolding the definitions of
o
and
i
, applying the functoriality
of
F_{\delta_{f}}
and comparing with the previous equality, one can simplify
into

T(w)\quad=\quad{\llparenthesis o_{f}\circ j_{f}\circ F_{\delta_{f}}\!\left(\widehat{g(w)}\right)\rrparenthesis}

It is now time to define
j_{f}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(F_{\delta_{f}}(\{\bullet\}),A\right)
.
To do so, let us first introduce

\mathsf{appto}(\varphi)\;:\;B\multimap C\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}(B\multimap C)\otimes\mathbf{I}\xrightarrow{\;\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi\;}(B\multimap C)\otimes B\xrightarrow{\;\mathrm{ev}\;}C

where the last arrow is the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}_{B,C}
, for any

B,C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
\varphi:\mathbf{I}\to B
. An useful property, whose
verification we leave to the reader, is

\mathsf{appto}(\varphi)\circ\Lambda^{\prime}(\psi)\quad=\quad\psi\circ\varphi\qquad\text{for any}\ \psi:B\to C

where
\Lambda^{\prime}:\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(B,C\right)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\mathbf{I},\,B\multimap C\right)
is defined in Proposition
10
. We then take

j_{f}\;:\;F_{\delta_{f}}(\{\bullet\})\xrightarrow{\;\pi_{1}\;}(A\multimap A)\multimap(A\multimap A)\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{appto}(\Lambda^{\prime}(\mathrm{id}_{A}))}A\multimap A\xrightarrow{\;\mathsf{appto}(i_{f})\;}A

where
\pi_{1}
is the left projection from
F_{\delta_{f}}(\{\bullet\})=((A\multimap A)\multimap(A\multimap A))\with\mathbf{I}
(this equality is guaranteed by
Corollary
22
) and
i_{f}
is the initialization morphism of

T_{f}
. Using the equation

F_{\delta_{f}}\!\left(\widehat{g(w)}\right)\quad=\quad\left\langle\Lambda^{\prime}\left(\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(g(w)_{1})\circ\dots\circ\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(g(w)_{m})\right),\;\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{I}}\right\rangle\quad\text{where}\ m={|g(w)|}

coming from Corollary
22
, we then have

j_{f}\circ F_{\delta_{f}}\!\left(\widehat{g(w)}\right)\quad=\quad\mathsf{appto}(i_{f})\circ\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(g(w)_{1})\circ\dots\circ\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(g(w)_{m})\circ\Lambda^{\prime}(\mathrm{id}_{A})

Next, we define
\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(c)=\Lambda(\mathrm{ev}_{A,A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A\multimap A}\otimes\delta_{f}(c)))\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A\multimap A,\;A\multimap A\right)
for
c\in\Gamma
. In other words,
\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(c)
is the curryfication of

(A\multimap A)\otimes A\xrightarrow{\;\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta_{f}(c)\;}(A\multimap A)\otimes A\xrightarrow{\;\mathrm{ev}\;}A

One can then check that
\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(c)\circ\Lambda^{\prime}(\psi)=\Lambda^{\prime}(\psi\circ(\delta_{f}(c)))
for any
\psi:A\to A
. Putting everything
together, we finally have

\displaystyle T(w)

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis o_{f}\circ\mathsf{appto}(i_{f})\circ\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(g(w)_{1})\circ\dots\circ\widetilde{\delta_{f}}(g(w)_{m})\circ\Lambda^{\prime}(\mathrm{id}_{A})\rrparenthesis}

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis o_{f}\circ\mathsf{appto}(i_{f})\circ\Lambda^{\prime}(\delta_{f}(g(w)_{m})\circ\dots\circ\delta_{f}(g(w)_{1}))\rrparenthesis}

\displaystyle={\llparenthesis o_{f}\circ\delta_{f}(g(w)_{m})\circ\dots\circ\delta_{f}(g(w)_{1})\circ i_{f}\rrparenthesis}

and this final expression is precisely the definition of the output of
T_{f}

on
g(w)
. Since
T_{f}
computes
f
, we end up with
T(w)=f(g(w))
, as we
wanted.

To conclude this section, let us note that an analogous result
for precomposition by regular tree functions can be shown by leveraging
the results of Section
5
; we leave it as an exercise. An important
subtlety: since the presence of the additive conjunction is important to compute
regular tree functions (as we stressed in the introduction), one must consider
tree streaming settings whose underlying categories
have finite cartesian
products
(which entails quasi-affineness).

4.2.
Uniformization through monoidal closure

We recall below the categorical uniformization theorem that we used in

Section

3.5
and provide its proof.
See
43

Recall that according to Lemma
41
, the conclusion
amounts to saying that non-deterministic sdm-
\mathfrak{C}
-SSTs are uniformizable by
partial sdm-
\mathfrak{D}
-SSTs.

Proof 4.4
.

We do not prove the statement in excruciating details, but provide key formal definitions
so that a reader familiar with a modicum of automata theory and category theory should be able
to reconstitute a fully formal argument with ease. Let us stress once again that
all of the combinatorics may be regarded as adaptation of known arguments.

The argument is based on a
notion of
transformation forest
, a name that we borrow from
[
BC18
, Chapter 13]
for an extremely
similar concept
22
22

22

There are two formal differences between our notions, which
are not very big but worth mentioning for readers of
[
BC18
]
.
First, edges of a transformation forest are intended to be associated
with (elements of) a monoid, while ours should be associated with (“elements of”) internal homsets

F(C_{u})\multimap F(C_{v})
. Were we trying to prove
\mathfrak{D}
-uniformization for
\mathfrak{C}
-SSTs, we
would have necessarily
C_{u}=C_{v}
and the aforementioned object would have a monoid structure
internal to
\mathcal{D}
, so this distinction is more an artefact of our settings rather than an essential one.
Second, what
[
BC18
]
calls transformation forests refers to a class of forest with “no junk”, such as
dangling leaves not referring to an intended output or spurious internal nodes, while we allow those in
an initial definition; we add the adjective “normalized” for those containing “no junk” as we shall
see later, so this is merely a terminological detail.
.
This gadget is also reminiscent of trees used in determinization procedures like
the Muller-Schupp construction for automata over

\omega
-words
[
MS95
]
(another exposition can be found in
[
BC18
, Chapter 1]
),
and of the sharing techniques used in the original paper on SSTs
[
AČ10
, §5.2]
.
In determinization procedures, this constitutes an elaboration of powerset constructions
recalling not only reachable states, but also crucial information on
how
those states are reached.
Here, the vertices
v
of such forests will be labelled by objects
C_{v}
of
\mathcal{C}
and each edge
(u,v)

will be correspond to a “register containing a value of type
F(C_{u})\multimap F(C_{v})
”.

We decompose this proof sketch in three parts: first, we introduce transformation forests,
their semantic interpretation as families of maps in
\mathcal{D}
; we explain how they may be composed
and that maps in
\mathcal{C}_{\with}
may be regarded as depth-1 transformation forests.
Then, we explain how to reduce the size of transformation forests in a sound way (this is the crucial
part ensuring that the resulting sdm-
\mathfrak{D}
-SSTs will have finitely many states). Finally, we explain how
to put all of this together to uniformize sdm-
\mathfrak{C}
-SSTs.

4.2.1.
Transformation forests and their semantics

A transformation forest is defined as a tuple
\mathcal{F}=(V,E,O,(C_{v})_{v\in V})
where

•

V
is a non-empty finite set of vertices

•

E\subseteq V^{2}
is a set of directed edges, pointing from parents to children

•

O
is a non-empty subset of
V

which we cal the set of
output nodes

•

every
C_{v}
is an object of
\mathcal{C}

When a transformation forest
\mathcal{F}
is fixed, we call
I_{\mathcal{F}}
its set of roots (which we may sometimes
input nodes
; we drop the subscript
when there is no ambiguity).
Given a transformation forest
\mathcal{F}=(V,E,(v_{o})_{o\in O},(C_{v})_{v\in V})
, we assign the following object of
\mathcal{D}
:

\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\quad=\quad\bigotimes_{(u,v)\in E}F(C_{u})\multimap F(C_{v})

An example of a transformation forest
\mathcal{F}
and a computation of its type
\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})
is pictured in Figure
9
.

Figure 9
.
A transformation forest
\mathcal{F}:T\with S\to\displaystyle\bigwith_{i=0}^{4}C_{i}
(
S,T,U,V,C_{0},\ldots,C_{4}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
).

To guide the intuition, one may note that there is an embedding
23
23

23

This becomes an isomorphism when
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\top,A\otimes B\right)\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\top,A\right)\times\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\top,B\right)
;
this is the case for our intended application
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{SR}_{\oplus}
.

of a set of suitable labellings of the forest
\mathcal{F}
into
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\top,\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\right)
.

\textstyle{\prod_{(u,v)\in E}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(F(C_{u}),F(C_{v})\right)\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\quad\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\top,\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\right)}

We will now call abusively the input of
\mathcal{F}
the object
A=\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}
and the output
B=\bigwith_{o\in O}C_{o}
; we write
\mathcal{F}:A\to B
in the sequel.
The justification for this notation is that there is a family of maps

{\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}\;\in\;\prod_{o\in O}\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F}),F(C_{i})\multimap F(C_{o})\right)

obtained by internalizing the composition of morphisms along branches of
\mathcal{F}
, which we call the
semantics of
\mathcal{F}
.

We also note that this allow to interpret arbitrary
\mathcal{C}_{\with}
morphisms: such a morphism
f:A\to B
can be mapped to a pair
(\mathrm{Grph}(f),\widehat{f})

consisting of

•

a depth-1 transformation forest
\mathrm{Grph}(f):\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}\to\bigwith_{o\in O}C_{o}
(an example is depicted in Figure
10
)

•

a morphism
\widehat{f}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\top,\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\right)

so that, if
f=(i_{o},\alpha_{o})_{o\in O}
, we have
{\llbracket\mathrm{Grph}(f)\rrbracket}_{o}\circ\widehat{f}=(i_{o},\Lambda(\alpha_{o}\circ\rho^{-1}))
.

Figure 10
.
A depth-
1
transformation forest.

Given two forests
\mathcal{F}:A\to B
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:B\to C
, there is a composition
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{F}

obtained by gluing the input nodes of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
along the output nodes of
\mathcal{F}
.
A crucial point is that the semantics of the composite
{\llbracket-\rrbracket}
may be computed as follows

{\llbracket\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}(o)\;=\;\left({\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}_{1}({\llbracket\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\rrbracket}_{1}(o^{\prime})),{\llbracket\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\rrbracket}_{2}(o^{\prime})\circ{\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}_{2}({\llbracket\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\rrbracket}_{1})\right)

4.2.2.
Reducing transformation forests

We now introduce two elementary transformations
\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
over transformation forests, together
with associated morphisms
\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
:

•

Pruning
: if
v\in V_{\mathcal{F}}
is a leaf which is not an output node in the forest
\mathcal{F}:A\to B
, call
\mathrm{prune}(\mathcal{F},v):A\to B
the forest obtained by
removing
v
and adjacent edges from
\mathcal{F}
.

This induces a canonical map

{prune}(v):\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Ty}(\mathrm{prune}(\mathcal{F},v))

by using the weakening maps on the components corresponding to the deleted edges.

•

Contraction
: If there is a vertex
v
with a unique child
c
and a (unique) parent
p
in the forest
\mathcal{F}:A\to B
, call
\mathrm{contract}(\mathcal{F},v):A\to B

the forest obtained by replacing the edges
(p,v)
and
(v,c)
with a single edge
(p,c)
and removing
v
.

There is a canonical map

{contract}(v):\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Ty}(\mathrm{contract}(\mathcal{F},v))

induced by the internal composition map

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left([F(C_{p})\multimap F(C_{v})]\otimes[F(C_{v})\multimap F(C_{c})],F(C_{p})\multimap F(C_{c})\right)

The auxiliary maps
{prune}
and
{contract}
operations are compatible with the semantic map
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}
in the sense
that for every
o\in O
and suitable vertices
u,v
of
\mathcal{F}
, we have

i=\pi_{1}({\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}(o))=\pi_{1}({\llbracket\mathrm{prune}(\mathcal{F},u)\rrbracket}(o))=\pi_{1}({\llbracket\mathrm{contract}(\mathcal{F},v)\rrbracket}(o))

and the following diagrams commute in
\mathcal{D}

\textstyle{\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}(o)}
\scriptstyle{{prune}(v)}
\textstyle{F(C_{i})\multimap F(C_{o})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ty}(\mathrm{prune}(\mathcal{F},v))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt{\llbracket\mathrm{prune}(\mathcal{F},v)\rrbracket}(o)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}(o)}
\scriptstyle{{contract}(v)}
\textstyle{F(C_{i})\multimap F(C_{o})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ty}(\mathrm{contract}(\mathcal{F},v))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt{\llbracket\mathrm{contract}(\mathcal{F},v)\rrbracket}(o)}

Consider the rewrite system
\leadsto
over forests
\mathcal{F}:A\to B
induced by those two operations, and call
\leadsto^{=}

its reflexive closure and
\leadsto^{*}
the transitive closure of
\leadsto^{=}
.
It can be shown that the reflexive closure
\leadsto^{=}
satisfies the
diamond lemma
, i.e.,
that for every
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathcal{F}\leadsto^{=}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}\leadsto^{=}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
, there exists
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\leadsto^{=}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\leadsto^{=}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime\prime}
. This ensures that
\leadsto
is confluent.
Furthermore, given a rewrite
\mathcal{F}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, there is a map
\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

obtained by composing maps
{prune}(v)
and
{contract}(v)

(and identities for trivial rewrites
\mathcal{F}\leadsto^{*}\mathcal{F}
).
It can be shown that this map does
not
depend on the rewrite path.
This is done first by arguing that if we have a rewrite square for
\leadsto^{=}
,
the associated diagram in
\mathcal{D}
is commutative, and then proceed by induction over the
rewrite paths using the diamond lemma.

\longmapsto\end{array}\qquad\qquad\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 75.72498pt\raise-31.96228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern Defining the size of a forest as its number of vertices, it is clear that
\mathrm{prune}(\mathcal{F},u)
and
\mathrm{contract}(\mathcal{F},v)

have size strictly less than
\mathcal{F}
, so the rewrite system is also terminating.
With confluence, it means that for every forest
\mathcal{F}:A\to B
, there is a unique forest
\mathrm{reduce}(\mathcal{F}):A\to B

such that
\mathcal{F}\leadsto^{*}\mathrm{reduce}(\mathcal{F})
and there is no
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{reduce}(\mathcal{F})\leadsto\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
.
We call
\mathrm{reduce}(\mathcal{F})
the
normal form
of
\mathcal{F}
; a forest
\mathcal{F}
is called
normal
if
\mathrm{reduce}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}
.
By the discussion above, there are canonical maps
reduce_{\mathcal{F}}:\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Ty}(\mathrm{reduce}(\mathcal{F}))
coming from rewrites.

Figure 11
.
An example of composing normal transformation forests, where first the usual composition and then two big steps of reduction, corresponding respectively to pruning and contracting, are carried out in succession.

The last important thing to note is that, if the input
A=\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}
and output
B=\bigwith_{o\in O}C_{o}
are fixed, up to isomorphism,
there are finitely many normal forests
\mathcal{F}:A\to B
as their size is bounded by
2({|I|}+{|O|})
.
We write
\mathrm{NF}\left({A},{B}\right)
for a finite set of representative for all normal forests
A\to B
, and given
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{NF}\left({B},{C}\right)
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{NF}\left({A},{B}\right)
,
we write
\mathcal{F}\circ_{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
for the unique forest in
\mathrm{NF}\left({A},{C}\right)
which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{reduce}(\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
; an example of the full computation of a
\mathcal{F}\circ_{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is given in Figure
11
.
Similarly, we assume that
\mathrm{Grph}(f)\in\mathrm{NF}\left({A},{B}\right)
for every morphism
f\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\with}}\left(A,B\right)
.

4.2.3.
Putting everything together

Let
\mathcal{T}=\left(Q,q_{0},\left(A_{q}\right)_{q\in Q},\delta,i,o\right)

be a sdm-
\mathfrak{C}_{\with}
-SST with input
\Sigma^{*}
. We define the sdm-
\mathfrak{D}
-SST

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\sum_{q\in Q}\mathrm{NF}\left({A_{q_{0}}},{A_{q}}\right),(q_{0},\mathrm{Grph}(\mathrm{id})),(\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F}))_{q,\mathcal{F}},\delta^{\prime},i^{\prime}o^{\prime}\right)

\begin{array}[]{c}\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \Sigma\to\prod_{q,\mathcal{F}}\sum_{r,\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F}),\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\right)\\

\mathrm{Ty}(\mathrm{Grph}(\mathrm{id}))=\bigotimes_{x\in X_{q_{0}}}F(C_{q_{0},x})\multimap F(C_{q_{0},x})

X_{q_{0}}
-fold tensor of
\Lambda(\mathrm{id}_{C_{q_{0},x}})
for
i^{\prime}
.

•

Fix
a\in\Sigma
and recall that
\delta(a)
is a family of pairs

(\delta^{Q}(a)_{q},\delta^{\mathcal{C}_{\with}}(a)_{q})_{q\in Q}\penalty\ \penalty\ \in\penalty\ \penalty\ \prod_{q\in Q}\sum_{r\in Q}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\with}}\left(A_{q},A_{r}\right)

\delta^{\prime}
is then defined by the equation

\delta^{\prime}(a)_{q,\mathcal{F}}\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\left(\delta^{Q}(a)_{q},\delta^{\mathrm{NF}}(a)_{q,\mathcal{F}}\right),\delta^{\mathcal{D}}(a)_{q,\mathcal{F}}\right)

where we set
\delta^{\mathrm{NF}}(a)_{q,\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{Grph}(\delta^{\mathcal{C}_{\with}}(a)_{q})\circ_{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{F}
and

\delta^{\mathcal{D}}(a)_{q,\mathcal{F}}
is obtained as in Figure
12

•

Finally, for
q,\mathcal{F}
ranging over states of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, we define
o^{\prime}_{q,\mathcal{F}}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{F}
determines a canonical family

{\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}\in\prod_{x\in X_{q}}\sum_{x_{0}\in X_{q_{0}}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathrm{Ty}(\mathcal{F}),F(C_{q_{0},x_{0}})\multimap F(C_{q,x})\right)

First, as
{}^{\mathfrak{C}_{\with}}=\iota_{\with}({}^{\mathfrak{C}})
, note that
there is a unique
x\in X_{q}
such that
o_{q}=(x,o^{\mathcal{C}})_{*}
for some
o^{\mathcal{C}}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{q,x},{}^{\mathfrak{C}}\right)
.
Writing the pair
{\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket}_{x}
as
(x_{0},f)
,

is depicted in Figure
12
.

We omit the proof that
{\llbracket\mathcal{T}\rrbracket}={\llbracket\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\rrbracket}
by induction
over the length of an input word.
While spelling it out may be a bit notation-heavy, there is no particular
difficulty considering the remarks above linking
{\llbracket-\rrbracket}
, the composition
of transformation forests and their normal forms.

5.
Regular tree functions in the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus

The goal of this section is now to prove our main theorem for tree functions
(that we recall below), extending the case of strings
(Theorem
1
).

See
2

To prove this theorem, we follow a similar approach as in Section
3
.

Remark 52
.

While the result on strings follows as a corollary of Theorem
2
,
this relies on the equivalence between regular string functions and regular tree functions when
strings are regarded as particular trees. Given that we start from transducer-based characterization
of these notions, this means that we would use e.g.
[
AD17
, Theorem 3.16-3.17]
, which are themselves
non-trivial results
24
24

24

A self-contained proof is also possible by building on our development
by exploiting Theorem
42
, arguably the most intricate argument presented here.
.

We thus first define a generalized notion of bottom-up ranked tree transducers (BRTT)
parameterized by a notion of
tree streaming setting
.

{defi}

Let
X
be a set.
A
tree streaming setting
with output
X

is a tuple
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbf{I},\Bot,{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis})
where

•

(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbf{I})
is a
symmetric monoidal
category

•

\Bot
is an object of
\mathcal{C}

•

{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis}
is a set-theoretic map
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\mathbf{I},\Bot\right)\to X

This notion essentially differs by asking that the underlying category be equipped
with a symmetric monoidal product, which is used in defining the semantics of

\mathfrak{C}
-BRTTs. The tensor product is used to fit the branching structure of trees
and
\mathbf{I}
is used for terminal nodes (so there is no need of a distinguished initial object

\Bot

as in string streaming settings).

{defi}

Let
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbf{I},\Bot,{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis})
be a tree streaming setting with output
X
.
A
\mathfrak{C}
-BRTT with input (ranked) alphabet
\bf\Sigma
and output
X
is a tuple

(Q,R,\delta,o)
where

•

Q
is a finite set of states

•

R
is an object of
\mathcal{C}

•

\delta
is a function
\displaystyle\prod_{a\in\Sigma}\left(Q^{\mathrm{ar}(a)}\to Q\times\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\bigotimes_{\mathrm{ar}(a)}R\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;R\right)\right)

•

o\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(R,\Bot\right)
is an output morphism

Its semantics is a set-theoretic map

\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to X
defined as follows: writing
\delta_{Q}(a,(t_{i})_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)})
for

\pi_{1}(\delta(a,(t_{i})_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}))
and
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(a,(t_{i})_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)})
for
\pi_{2}(\delta(a,(t_{i})_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}))
, define auxiliary functions
\delta^{*}_{Q}:\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to Q

and
\delta^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\mathbf{I},R\right)
by iterating
\delta
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\delta_{Q}^{*}\left(a\left((t_{i})_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}\right)\right)&\penalty\ \penalty\ =&\delta_{Q}\left(a,(\delta^{*}_{Q}(t_{i}))_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}\right)\\
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\left(a\left((t_{i})_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}\right)\right)&\penalty\ \penalty\ =&\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{C}}\left(a,(\delta^{*}_{Q}(t_{i}))_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}\right)\circ\left(\bigotimes_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}\delta^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(t_{i})\right)\circ\varphi_{\mathrm{ar}(a)}\end{array}

where
\varphi_{\mathrm{ar}(a)}
is the unique isomorphism

\mathbf{I}\xrightarrow{\,\sim\,}\bigotimes_{i\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}\mathbf{I}

generated by the associator and unitors of
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbf{I})
.
The output function
{\llbracket\mathcal{T}\rrbracket}:\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to X
is defined as
{\llbracket\mathcal{T}\rrbracket}={\llparenthesis o\circ\delta^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}\rrparenthesis}
.

Remark 53
.

Strictly speaking, we do not need the monoidal product to be symmetric for the notion
to make sense, but it would require using a fixed order over the input ranked alphabet
\bf\Sigma
.
Although one could choose an arbitrary total order over
\bf\Sigma
, different orders might define different classes of
functions
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
when the monoidal product is not symmetric. This
is why we work in symmetric monoidal categories.

As with string streaming settings, it is convenient to define a notion of morphism
of tree streaming settings to compare the expressiveness of classes of BRTTs.

{defi}

Let
\mathfrak{C}=(\mathcal{C},\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{C}},\otimes_{\mathcal{C}},{}_{\mathcal{C}},{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis}_{\mathfrak{C}})

and

\mathfrak{D}=(\mathcal{D},\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{D}},\otimes_{\mathcal{C}},{}_{\mathcal{D}},{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis}_{\mathfrak{D}})

be tree streaming settings with output
X
. A morphism of tree streaming settings is given by
a lax monoidal functor
F:(\mathcal{C},\otimes_{\mathcal{C}},\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{C}})\to(\mathcal{D},\otimes_{\mathcal{D}},\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{D}})

and a
\mathcal{D}
-arrow
o:F({}_{\mathcal{C}})\to{}_{\mathcal{D}}

such that, for every
f\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\text{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\Bot$}}}}_{\mathcal{C}},{}_{\mathcal{C}}\right)
, we have

{\llparenthesis o\circ F(f)\circ i\rrparenthesis}_{\mathcal{D}}={\llparenthesis f\rrparenthesis}_{\mathcal{C}}

where
i:\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{D}}\to F(\mathbf{I}_{\mathcal{C}})
is obtained as part of the lax monoidal functor structure over
F
.

Observe that we do not require those functors to commute with the symmetry
morphisms for the monoidal products, as promised in
Section

2.4
.
This is consistent with the fact that the symmetries are not really involved in
computing the image of a tree by a
\mathfrak{C}
-BRTT, according to
Remark
53
: it is only their mere existence that matters.

Lemma 54
.

If there is a morphism of tree streaming settings
\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
, then
\mathfrak{D}
-BRTTs subsume
\mathfrak{C}
-BRTTs.

Proof 5.1
.

Let us give the proof for single-state BRTTs; the proof for general BRTTs would be notationally heavier
but not much more insightful.
Suppose that we have some single-state
\mathfrak{C}
-BRTT
\mathcal{T}=(R,\delta,o)
– where
we leave the only state implicit and regard the transition function
\delta
and
output function
o
as elements of

\prod_{a\in\Sigma}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\bigotimes_{\mathrm{ar}(a)}R\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;R\right)
and

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(R,\Bot\right)
respectively –
and that the morphism under consideration is composed of a lax monoidal functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}

and a
\mathcal{D}
-arrow
o^{\prime}:F({}_{\mathcal{C}})\to{}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Since
F
is lax monoidal, we have a family of natural transformations

m_{I,A}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bigotimes_{I}F(A)\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\left(\bigotimes_{I}A\right)

where
I
ranges over all finite sets
25
25

25

Recall from

Section

2.1
that an operation
\bigotimes_{i\in I}(-)
– here,
a functor – is associated to every finite indexing set
I
by choosing an
arbitrary total order over
I
.
and
A
over objects of
\mathcal{C}
; this family is
compatible with the associators and unitors in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
. Furthermore,

m_{\varnothing,A}:\mathbf{I}\to F(\mathbf{I})
is the same for all
A\in\mathsf{Obj}(C)
, so
we shall abbreviate it as
m_{\varnothing}
.

We claim that
{\llbracket\mathcal{T}\rrbracket}={\llbracket\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\rrbracket}
, where
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is the single-state
\mathfrak{D}
-BRTT
(F(R),\delta^{\prime},o^{\prime}\circ F(o))
with the same typing conventions and

\delta^{\prime}_{a}=F(\delta_{a})\circ m_{\mathrm{ar}(a),R}
.
Let us prove this. To do so we first consider the iterations of the transition functions

\delta^{*}:\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\mathbf{I},R\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta^{\prime*}:\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathbf{I},F(R)\right)

and show that
\delta^{\prime*}(t)=F(\delta(t))\circ m_{\varnothing}
by induction over
t\in\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
.
So suppose that
t=a((u_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(a)})
and that the inductive
hypothesis holds (this also takes care of the base case: when

\mathrm{ar}(a)=\varnothing
, the inductive hypothesis is vacuous). In such a case, let
us show that each face in the following diagram commutes (where all tensor products have arity
\mathrm{ar}(a)
):

\textstyle{\mathbf{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{(b)}}
\scriptstyle{m_{\varnothing}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime*}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{(a)}}
\textstyle{\bigotimes\mathbf{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigotimes m_{\varnothing}}
\scriptstyle{\bigotimes\limits_{x}\delta^{\prime*}(u_{x})}
\textstyle{\bigotimes F(\mathbf{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\bigotimes\limits_{x}F(\delta^{*}(u_{x}))}
\scriptstyle{m_{\mathrm{ar}(a),\mathbf{I}}}
\textstyle{\bigotimes F(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{m_{\mathrm{ar}(a),R}}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{(e)}}
\textstyle{F(R)}
\textstyle{F\left(\bigotimes\mathbf{I}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{(d)}}
\scriptstyle{F\left(\bigotimes\limits_{x}\delta^{*}(u_{x})\right)}
\textstyle{F\left(\bigotimes R\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\delta_{a})}
\textstyle{F(\mathbf{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathtt{(f)}}
\scriptstyle{F(\sim)}
\scriptstyle{F(\delta^{*}(t))}

Faces
(a)
and
(f)
commute by definition of iterated transition functions and
face
(e)
corresponds to the definition of
\delta^{\prime}
. Face
(d)
commutes because
of the naturality of
m_{-,-}
and face
(b)
because of its compatibility with associators.
Finally, face
(c)
corresponds to
the inductive hypothesis.
Therefore, the topmost and bottommost paths coincide, so we have
\delta^{\prime*}(t)=F(\delta(t))\circ m_{\varnothing}
,
which concludes our inductive argument. We can then conclude since we have, for every tree
t
,

\begin{array}[]{lcll}{\llbracket\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\rrbracket}(t)&=&{\llparenthesis o^{\prime}\circ F(o)\circ(\delta^{\prime*}(t))\rrparenthesis}_{\mathfrak{D}}&\text{by definition}\\
&=&{\llparenthesis o^{\prime}\circ F(o)\circ F(\delta^{*}(t))\circ m_{\varnothing}\rrparenthesis}_{\mathfrak{D}}&\text{inductive argument}\\
&=&{\llparenthesis o^{\prime}\circ F(o\circ\delta^{*}(t))\circ m_{\varnothing}\rrparenthesis}_{\mathfrak{D}}&\text{by functoriality}\\
&=&{\llparenthesis o\circ(\delta^{*}(t))\rrparenthesis}_{\mathfrak{C}}&\text{since $(F,o^{\prime})$ is part of a morphism $\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}$}\\
&=&{\llbracket\mathcal{T}\rrbracket}(t)&\text{by definition}\\
\end{array}

From now on, we may omit the “tree” in when discussing streaming settings.

As for SSTs, linearity is an important concept for traditional BRTTs over ranked alphabets.
Rather than starting from the category corresponding exactly to usual BRTTs, we will first study a
more convenient, albeit less expressive, streaming setting based on the idea of trees with multiple holes.
For this, it will be convenient to introduce the notion of
multicategory
, which is essentially
a notion of category where morphisms are allowed to have multiple input objects.
We will thus first devote Section
5.1
to some preliminaries describing how
to (freely) generate affine monoidal categories from multicategories.

After spelling out the universal properties that will allow us to easily define functors and
a quick review of the generalization of the results of Section
3.4
pertaining to
coproduct completions in Section
5.2
, we present a basic multicategory of registers

\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
containing “trees with holes” in Section
5.3
, and the corresponding
streaming setting
\mathfrak{TR}
on top of a category
\mathcal{TR}
.
The usual restriction to registers containing “trees with at most one hole”
is also discussed and shown to be no less expressive thanks to our basic results on the coproduct completion.
Then, we explain how the usual notion of BRTT presented in
[
AD17
]
can be shown to have the same expressiveness
as
\mathfrak{TR}_{\with}
-BRTTs in Section
5.4
.

Finally, in Section
5.5
, we show that
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
has internal homsets
\iota_{\oplus}(R)\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(S)
and conclude that
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}
is monoidal-closed. We conclude the
proof of Theorem
2
in Section
5.7
.

For the rest of this part, we fix a ranked alphabet
\bf\Gamma
so that we may focus on outputs
contained in
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Gamma})
, much like we focussed on outputs in some fixed
\Gamma^{*}
before.

5.1.
Multicategorical preliminaries

This section is devoted to spelling out the formal definition of the notion of
multicategory that we use in the sequel, and their relation to symmetric
affine monoidal category. While technically necessary for the sequel, it is rather
dry and should maybe only be skimmed over at first reading.

{defi}

A (weak symmetric) multicategory
\mathcal{M}
consists of

•

a class of objects
\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{M})

•

a class of
multimorphisms
going from pairs
(I,(A_{i})_{i\in I})
of a finite index set
I
and a family
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects to
objects
B
. We omit the first component of the source and write
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)
for the set of these multimorphisms.

•

for every object
A
, a distinguished identity multimorphism
\mathrm{id}_{A}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A)_{*\in 1},A\right)
.

•

for every set-theoretic map
f:I\to J
, families
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(B_{j})_{j\in J}
and object
C
, a composition operation

\begin{array}[]{ccccl}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((B_{j})_{j\in J},C\right)&\times&\left[\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in f^{-1}(j)},B_{j}\right)\right]&\longrightarrow&\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},C\right)\\
\alpha&,&(\beta_{j})_{j\in J}&\longmapsto&\alpha*_{f}\beta\end{array}

•

for every bijection
\sigma:I^{\prime}\to I
between finite sets, a family of actions

\sigma^{*}:\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)\to\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{\sigma(i^{\prime})})_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}},B\right)

correspond to reindexing along symmetries.

Furthermore, the above data is required to obey the following laws.

•

The identity morphism be a neutral for composition:
for any
\alpha\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)
,

\mathrm{id}_{B}*_{!}(\alpha)_{*\in 1}\quad=\quad\alpha\quad=\quad\alpha*_{\mathrm{id}_{I}}(\mathrm{id}_{A_{i}})_{i\in I}

•

Composition is associative: for any finite sets
I,J
and
K
, functions
f:K\to J
and
g:J\to I
,
families of objects
(A_{k})_{k\in K}
,
(B_{j})_{j\in J}
,
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
,
D
and families of morphisms

\begin{array}[]{l}\alpha\in\prod_{j\in J}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{k})_{k\in f^{-1}(j)},B_{j}\right)\\
\beta\in\prod_{i\in I}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((B_{j})_{j\in g^{-1}(i)},C_{i}\right)\\
\gamma\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((C_{i})_{i\in I},D\right)\end{array}

the following equation holds

\gamma*_{g}\left(\beta*_{f}\alpha\right)=\left(\gamma*_{g\upharpoonright f^{-1}(j)}\beta\right)_{j}*_{f}\alpha

•

Permutations act functorially: for any
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
,
B
and bijections
\sigma:I^{\prime}\to I
and

\sigma^{\prime}:I^{\prime\prime}\to I^{\prime}
, the following commute

\textstyle{\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{\sigma(i^{\prime})})_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}},B\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{\sigma(\sigma^{\prime}(i^{\prime\prime}))})_{i^{\prime\prime}\in I^{\prime\prime}},B\right)}

and
\mathrm{id}_{I}^{*}:\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)\to\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)
is the identity.

•

Composition is compatible with permutations: for every commuting square

\textstyle{J^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{I^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{I}

in
{\sf FinSet}
such that the vertical arrows be bijections, note in particular that for
every
i\in I
, there is a bijection
\sigma_{i}:g^{-1}(I^{\prime})\to f^{-1}(I)
; we thus require that

\alpha*_{f}\left(\beta_{j\in f^{-1}(i)}\right)_{i\in I}\quad=\quad\sigma^{*}(\alpha)*_{g}\left(\sigma_{i}^{*}\left(\beta_{j\in f^{-1}(i)}\right)\right)_{i\in I}

for every suitable
\alpha
and
\beta_{j}
s.

Every symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
can be mapped to a multicategory
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{mcat}}

by taking

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{mcat}}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)\quad=\quad\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;B\right)

We may make this map functorial, provided we equip the class of multicategories
and the class of symmetric monoidal categories with categorical structures.

{defi}

Given two weak multicategories
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
, a functor
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
consists
of maps of objects
F:\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{N})
and of multimorphisms

F\penalty\ :\penalty\ \mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)\penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{N}}\left((F(A_{i}))_{i\in I},F(B)\right)

such that
F(\mathrm{id}_{A})=\mathrm{id}_{F(A)}
,
F\left(\alpha*_{f}\left(\beta_{j}\right)_{j\in f^{-1}(i)}\right)=F(\alpha)*_{f}(F(\beta_{j}))_{j\in f^{-1}(i)}
and
F(\sigma*(\alpha))=\sigma^{*}(F(\alpha))
for all suitable objects, index sets, set-theoretic functions and morphisms.

Definition
5.1
gives a class of arrows for a large category
\sf MCat
of multicategories.
Calling
\sf Aff
the category whose objects are symmetric affine monoidal categories and morphisms are
strong monoidal functors, the map
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{mcat}}
extends to a functor
\sf Aff\to\sf MCat
.
We are now interested in the inverse process of generating freely a symmetric affine monoidal
category out of a weak multicategory.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a weak multicategory.
The
free affine symmetric monoidal category
generated by
\mathcal{M}
is the category
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}
such that

•

objects are pairs
(I,(A_{i})_{i\in I})
of a finite set
I
and a family of objects of
\mathcal{M}
; write
\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}
for such objects.

•

morphisms
\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\bigotimes_{j\in J}B_{j}
are pairs
(f,(\alpha_{j})_{j\in J})
where
f
is a partial function
I\rightharpoonup J
and

\alpha_{j}
is a
\mathcal{M}
multimorphism
(A_{i})_{i\in f^{-1}(J)}\to B_{j}
.

•

identities
\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}
are the pairs
(\mathrm{id}_{I},(\mathrm{id}_{A_{i}})_{i\in I})
.

•

the composition of

(f,(\alpha_{j})_{j\in J}):\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\bigotimes_{j\in J}B_{j}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ (g,(\beta_{k})_{k\in K}):\bigotimes_{j\in J}B_{j}\to\bigotimes_{k\in K}C_{k}

is

\left(g\circ f,\left(\beta_{k}*\left(\alpha_{j}\right)_{j\in g^{-1}(k)}\right)_{k\in K}\right)
.

For any bijection
\sigma:I^{\prime}\to I
, we have canonical isomorphisms
\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{\sigma(i)}
. We take the binary tensor product to be

\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{j\in J}B_{j}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigotimes_{x\in I+J}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{i}&\text{if $x=\mathsf{in}_{1}(i)$}\\
B_{j}&\text{if $x=\mathsf{in}_{2}(j)$}\\
\end{array}\right.

and the unit to be the terminal object, which is the nullary family
\bigotimes_{\varnothing}
.
The associator and symmetries are induced by the isomorphisms
(I+J)+K\cong I+(J+K)
and
I+J\cong J+I

respectively, and the units by
I+\varnothing\cong I\cong\varnothing+I
. The axioms of weak symmetric multicategories then imply that this indeed endows
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}
with a symmetric affine monoidal structure.
We skip checking the details.

5.2.
The coproduct completion

Similarly as for strings, the coproduct completion of a category induces a map

\mathfrak{C}\mapsto\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus}
over tree streaming settings.
Furthermore, expressiveness of
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus}
remain the same under similar hypotheses as Theorem
31
.

Theorem 55
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a tree streaming setting whose monoidal product is affine and such that
all objects of the underlying category have unitary support. Then,
\mathfrak{C}
-BRTTs and
\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus}
-BRTTs

are equi-expressive.

The proof is an unsurprising adaptation of the one of Theorem
31
.
We leave it to the interested reader.
Similarly, we define the notion of a state-dependent memory
\mathfrak{C}
-streaming tree transducer (sdm-
\mathfrak{C}
-BRTT),
which can be shown to be as expressive as sdm-
\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus}
-BRTT. We only state the definition and
the generalization of Lemma
33
, whose proof we defer to the interested reader.

{defi}

A
\mathfrak{C}
-
state-dependent memory BRTT

with input
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
is a tuple
(Q,\delta,(C_{q})_{q\in Q},o)
where

•

Q
is a finite set of states

•

(C_{q})_{q\in Q}
is a
Q
-indexed family of objects of
\mathcal{C}

•

\displaystyle\delta\in\left[\prod_{a\in\Sigma}\prod_{q\in Q^{\mathrm{ar}(a)}}\sum_{r\in Q}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(a)}C_{q(x)},C_{r}\right)\right]
is a transition function

•

\displaystyle o\in\prod_{q\in Q}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{q},\Bot\right)
is the output family of morphisms

Lemma 56
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a streaming setting. State-dependent memory
\mathfrak{C}
-BRTTs are as expressive as
\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus}
-BRTTs.

5.3.
The combinatorial multicategory
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}

We are now ready to give a smooth definition of a category of register transition
for trees, generalizing Proposition
16
.
As announced, we find it more convenient to first give a multicategory
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}

and then move to monoidal categories by taking
\mathcal{TR}=(\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathrm{aff}}
.
We then discuss the restriction consisting of limiting the number of holes in the tree
expressions stored in register to at most one and show that it is not limiting.

Recall that we regard ranked alphabets
\bf R
as pairs
(R,\mathrm{ar})
where
R
is a finite
set of letters and
(\mathrm{ar}(a))_{a\in R}
is a family
R\to{\sf FinSet}
of arities.
Given two ranked alphabets
\bf R
and
\bf S
, we suggestively
write
\bf R\otimes\bf S
for the ranked alphabet
(R+S,[\mathrm{ar},\mathrm{ar}])
.
Given a finite set
U
, call
\mathcal{O}(U)
the ranked alphabet
(U,(\varnothing)_{u\in U})
consisting
of
{|U|}
-many terminal letters and
\mathcal{I}(U)
for the ranked alphabet consisting of a single letter of arity
U
.
Given a ranked alphabet
{\bf\Sigma}=(\Sigma,\mathrm{ar})
and a subset
X\subseteq\Sigma
, we write
{\bf\Sigma}\upharpoonright X
for the restriction
(X,\mathrm{ar}\upharpoonright X)
.

5.3.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{TR}

Before giving the definition of
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
, we first need to make formal a notion
of trees with linearly many occurrences of certain constructors.

{defi}

Let
\bf R
be a ranked alphabet. We define the set
\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}({\bf R})

of
\bf R
-linear trees as the set of
({\bf\Gamma}\otimes{\bf R})
-trees
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Gamma}\otimes{\bf R})
such that all constructors of
\bf R
appear exactly once.

{defi}

Define
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}({\bf\Gamma})
(abbreviated
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
in the sequel) as the multicategory

•

whose class of objects
\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}})
is
{\sf FinSet}
.

•

whose class of multimorphisms from
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
to
B

is the set of linear trees over the joint alphabet
(I,A)\otimes\mathcal{O}(B)

(recall that
(I,A)
can formally be regarded as a ranked alphabet: its set of
letters is
I
and the arity of
i\in I
is
A(i)=A_{i}
).

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}}\left((A_{i})_{i\in I},B\right)\quad=\quad\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}((I,A)\otimes\mathcal{O}(B))

•

whose composition operations are given by substitution: given a map
f:I\to J
and multimorphisms

t\in\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}((J,B)\otimes\mathcal{O}(C))\qquad\text{and}\qquad u\in\prod_{j\in J}\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}((f^{-1}(j),(A_{i})_{i})\otimes\mathcal{O}(B_{j}))

the composite
t*_{f}u
is defined by recursion over
t
:

–

if
t=a((t^{\prime}_{k})_{k})
for some
a=\mathsf{in}_{1}(b)
with
b\in\Gamma
or
a=\mathsf{in}_{2}(\mathsf{in}_{2}(c))
for
c\in C
, then

t*_{f}u=a((t^{\prime}_{k}*_{f}u)_{k})

–

otherwise
t=\mathsf{in}_{2}(\mathsf{in}_{1}(j))((t^{\prime}_{b})_{b\in B_{j}})
with
j\in J
and
t^{\prime}_{b}
in some
\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}((J_{b},B_{b})\otimes\mathcal{O}(C_{j}))
for
\bigcup_{b\in B_{j}}J_{b}=J\setminus\{j\}
,
B_{b}=B\upharpoonright J_{b}
and
\bigcup_{b\in B_{j}}C_{b}=C
. In such a case, we set

t*_{f}u=u_{j}[(t^{\prime}_{b}*_{\mathrm{id}_{J_{b}}}(u_{j^{\prime}})_{j^{\prime}\in J_{b}})/b]_{b\in B_{j}}

where
[-/-]_{-\in-}
denotes the more usual substitution of leaves by subtrees (recall that every
b\in B
, and a fortiori
B_{j}
has arity
\varnothing
).

While the definition of composition of multimorphisms in
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}

looks daunting, we claim it is rather natural. Figure
13

depicts the composition
\alpha*_{f}(\beta_{x})_{x\in\{t,u\}}
with

\alpha=t(a(u(c()),*()),c())\qquad\beta_{t}=a(x(p(),b(q())),y())\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{u}=z(c(),r(),c())

Figure 13.
Composition of some multimorphisms of
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
.

We now set
\mathcal{TR}=(\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathrm{aff}}
;
while objects of
(\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathrm{aff}}
are supposed to be families of finite sets
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
,
in the sequel, we sometimes identify them with the ranked alphabets
(I,A)
in
\mathcal{TR}
for notational convenience.
As such, the notation
{\bf R}\otimes{\bf S}
corresponds to the expected tensorial product in
\mathcal{TR}
.

We are now ready to define our first tree streaming setting.

{defi}

\mathfrak{TR}
is the tree streaming setting
(\mathcal{TR},\otimes,\top,\mathcal{I}(\varnothing),{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis})
, where
{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis}
is the canonical isomorphism

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}}\left(\top,\mathcal{I}(\varnothing)\right)\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}}\left(()_{\varnothing},\varnothing\right)\cong\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}(\varnothing)\cong\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Gamma})

(where
()_{\varnothing}
is the empty family).

Call
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m},\leq 1}
the full submulticategory of
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
whose objects
are empty or singleton sets, and

\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}\cong\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m},\leq 1}_{\mathrm{aff}}
to be the corresponding full subcategory of
\mathcal{TR}
.
The monoidal structure of
\mathcal{TR}
restricts to
\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}
without any difficulty, and that
\mathcal{I}(\varnothing)
is an
object of
\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}
. This means that
\mathfrak{TR}
has a restriction to a streaming setting
\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}
.

While
\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}
turns out to be more elementary and a good building block toward the definition of
usual BRTTs, it is easier to show the monoidal closure of
\mathfrak{TR}_{\oplus\with}
than
\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}
.
Thankfully, it turns out that the expressiveness of BRTTs over
\mathfrak{TR}
and

\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}
is the same.

For one direction, there is a morphism
\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}\to\mathfrak{TR}
corresponding to the embedding
\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}\to\mathcal{TR}
.
For the other direction, we exploit Theorem
55
.
The proof involves some combinatorics, but nothing surprising as it
amounts to the classical decomposition of multi-hole trees into families of single-hole trees as found in e.g.
[
AD17
, §3.5]
.

Lemma 57
.

There is a morphism of streaming settings
\mathfrak{TR}\to\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}
.

Proof 5.2

(Proof sketch)
.

We focus on giving enough ingredients to define the underlying (strong) monoidal functor
F:\mathcal{TR}\to\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}
,
which is going to preserve
\Bot
(i.e., we will have
F(\mathcal{I}(\varnothing))\cong\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(\varnothing))
).

Rather than giving a direct explicit construction of
F
(which is rather tedious over morphisms), we obtain
it as a composition of two strong monoidal functors:
the strong monoidal embedding
\iota_{\oplus}:\mathcal{TR}\to\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
and a functor

R:\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}\to\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}
right adjoint to
the inclusion
I:\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}\to\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
.

\textstyle{\mathcal{TR}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\oplus}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}

I
is strong symmetric monoidal. Therefore,
by
[
Mel09
, Proposition 14, Section 5.17]
, once we construct

R
right adjoint to
I
, it comes equipped with a canonical lax monoidal structure.
Furthermore, since we want
I\dashv R
, we can use the implicit characterization of adjoints
given in
[
ML98
, item (iv), Theorem 2, Section IV.1]
:
to define
R
,
it suffices to give the value of
R\left(A\right)
for every object
A\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus})

and counit maps
\epsilon_{A}:I(R(A))\to A
such that, for every object
B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus})

and map
h\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}}\left(I(B),A\right)
, there is a unique
\widetilde{h}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}}\left(R(A),B\right)
such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{I(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{I(\widetilde{h})}
\textstyle{I(R(A))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{A}}
\textstyle{A}

So we only need to define
R(A)
and
\epsilon_{A}
to obtain our functor
R
; once those are defined,
we leave checking that the universal property holds to the reader.
We first focus on the case where
A=\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))
for some finite set
U
.
Recall that a single-letter alphabet
\mathcal{I}(U)
, when seen as an object of
\mathcal{TR}
, should be
should be intuitively regarded as a register containing a tree with
U
-many holes.
If
{|U|}\leq 1
, we may simply take
R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))=\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))
.
Otherwise,
{|U|}\geq 2
and
\mathcal{I}(U)
is not an object
\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}
; in that case,
we use the following recursive definition

R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(1)\penalty\ \penalty\ \otimes\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus\limits_{b\in\Gamma}\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:U\to\mathrm{ar}(b)\\
\text{nonconstant}\end{subarray}}\bigotimes\limits_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))))

Note that this definition is well-founded because the function
f
in the second sum is taken to be non constant, so
that
{|f^{-1}(x)|}<{|U|}
for every
x
. While this suffices as a definition of
R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))
, this
might be a bit opaque without having the definition of
\epsilon_{R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))}
. Before giving that, let us attempt to give
an intuitive rationale behind this definition: there is an isomorphism
26
26

26

Which we may later on define formally as a composite

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}}\left(\top,R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))\right)\xrightarrow{\;I\;}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}}\left(\top,R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))\right)\to\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}}\left(\top,\mathcal{I}(U)\right)\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}(U)

where the mediating arrow is the post-composition by
\epsilon_{\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))}
.

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}}\left(\top,R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))\right)\cong\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}(U)

which can be nicely pictured, provided we actually compute recursively
R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))
and spell out a normal form

R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \cong\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{t\in{\bf PT}(U)}\bigotimes_{n\in N(t)}A_{n}

with all
A_{n}=\mathcal{I}(\varnothing)
or
A_{n}\cong\mathcal{I}(1)
. It is always possible to build a suitable set
{\bf PT}(U)
simply because
all objects of
\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}
have this shape, but an intuitive definition of what one might call a set of

partitioning trees over
U
is also possible for
\bf PT
, and
N(t)
would then correspond to the nodes of the trees.
We skip defining this notion formally, but note that the announced bijection would then match trees with
U
-many holes with
pairs
(t,(u_{i})_{i\in N(t)})
of a partitioning tree
t
and a family of trees with at most one-hole
(u_{i})_{i\in N(t)}
.
This bijective correspondence is pictured in Figure

.

Figure 14
.
Decomposition of a multi-hole tree

as a tuple consisting of a partitioning tree and trees with at most one hole.

Now, we define
\epsilon_{R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus}}\left(I(R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))),\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))\right)
, by induction over the
size of
U
. If
{|U|}\leq 1
, we take
