to be the identity.
Otherwise, we need to define a map

g_{U}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ I\left(\bigoplus_{b\in\Gamma}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:U\to\mathrm{ar}(b)\\
\text{nonconstant}\end{subarray}}\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))))\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))

or, equivalently, a family of
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
-maps indexed by
b\in\Gamma
and
f:U\to\mathrm{ar}(b)
non-constant

g_{b,f}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}I(R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x)))))\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))

By the induction hypothesis, we have a family of
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
-maps
(g_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}

g_{x}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ I(R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x)))))\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x)))

We define
g_{b,f}
as the composite

\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}I(R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x)))))\penalty\ \xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigotimes_{x}g_{x}\penalty\ \penalty\ }\penalty\ \displaystyle\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x)))\penalty\ \xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ \overline{b}\penalty\ \penalty\ }\penalty\ \iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))

where
\overline{b}
is obtained from the map of
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
which intuitively takes a family
(t_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}
of trees into a
single tree
b((t_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)})
(officially, the tree
b((*)_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)})\in\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}(\mathcal{O}(U))
.

Now,
R
is defined on objects of the shape
\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))
, as well as
\epsilon
, so we need to extend this to the whole category

\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
. Recall that every object
A
of
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
can be written as
A=\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigotimes_{j\in J_{u}}\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U_{j}))
.
In the end, the functor
R
is expected to be strong monoidal, and we may force it to preserve coproducts, so we set

R(A)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigotimes_{j\in J_{u}}R(\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U_{j})))\qquad\qquad\qquad\epsilon_{A}\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigotimes_{j\in J_{u}}\epsilon_{\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U_{j}))}

5.3.2.
Relationship to
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}

Recall that if
{\bf\Gamma}=\{a_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,a_{k}:A_{k}\}
is an output alphabet,
we call
\widetilde{\bf\Gamma}
the context
a_{1}:\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o},\ldots,a_{k}:\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}
where the
type of
a_{i}
has
{|A_{i}|}
arguments. Definition
3.3
provides us with a suitable affine monoidal closed category

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\bf\Gamma})
, which we still call
\mathcal{L}
when
\widetilde{\bf\Gamma}
is clear from context. Since we have a monoidal product,
we may easily adapt Definition
3.3
to get a tree streaming setting
\mathfrak{L}
.
Then we may relate
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-definability to (single-state)
\mathfrak{L}
-BRTT.

Lemma 58
.

Computability by single-state
\mathfrak{L}
-BRTTs and
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-definability are
equivalent for functions
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Gamma})
.

Proof 5.3

(Proof idea)
.

This is proven in a similar way as Lemma
24
, based on the syntactic Lemma
25
.
The proof is even more straightforward as there is no mismatch
between the processing of trees by BRTTs and
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms working with Church encodings, contrary to
SSTs for strings (which operate top-down rather than bottom-up when regarding strings as trees).

We can now notice that
\mathfrak{L}
-BRTTs are more expressive than
\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}
-BRTTs thanks to the notion of streaming setting morphisms,
much like with strings (this generalizes Lemma
26
).

Lemma 59
.

There is a morphism of streaming settings
\mathfrak{TR}\to\mathfrak{L}
.

Proof 5.4

(Proof sketch)
.

Let us focus on the underlying functor
F:\mathcal{TR}\to\mathcal{L}
.
For objects (which are finite sets), we
put

F\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}U_{i}\right)=\bigotimes_{i\in I}\left(\left(\mathbbl{o}^{\otimes U_{i}}\multimap\mathbbl{o}\right)\with\mathbf{I}\right)

A multimorphism
f\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}}\left((U_{i})_{i\in I},V\right)
is an element of
\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}\left(\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}\mathcal{I}(U_{i})\right)\otimes\mathcal{O}(V)\right)

which has a Church encoding
\underline{f}
which has a type isomorphic to

\bigotimes_{i\in I}\left(\mathbbl{o}^{\otimes{U_{i}}}\multimap\mathbbl{o}\right)\multimap\mathbbl{o}
, and thus embeds into

F\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}U_{i}\right)
through well-typed term
\iota
.
We take
F(f_{\mathrm{aff}})=\lambda x.\iota\;\underline{f}
, and extend this definition to
arbitrary morphisms
(f,(\alpha_{j})_{j}):\bigotimes_{i\in I}U_{i}\to\bigotimes_{j\in J}V_{j}
in
\mathcal{TR}
by first using the second projection
\pi_{2}
to restrict to the case where
{\sf{dom}}(f)=I
, and then by piecing together the
F((\alpha_{j})_{\mathrm{aff}})
.

Corollary 60
.

There is a morphism of streaming settings
\mathfrak{TR}_{\oplus\with}\to\mathfrak{L}
.

Proof 5.5

(Proof idea)
.

Starting from Lemma
59
, we have a functor
\mathcal{TR}\to\mathcal{L}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
has all products and
coproducts, the universal properties of the
(-)_{\with}
and
(-)_{\oplus}
completion yield a functor
F:\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}\to\mathcal{L}
.
The monoidal structure of the initial functor
\mathcal{TR}\to\mathcal{L}
can be lifted accordingly.
For any finite family of objects
(((A_{k})_{k\in K_{j}})_{j\in J_{i}})_{i\in I}
sitting in a symmetric monoidal closed category with
products and coproducts, there are canonical morphisms

\left(\bigoplus_{u\in U}\bigwith_{x\in X_{u}}A_{x}\right)\otimes\left(\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{v\in Y_{v}}B_{y}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{(u,v)\in U\times V}\bigwith_{(x,y)\in X_{u}\times Y_{v}}A_{x}\otimes B_{y}

which are not isomorphisms in general, but constitute the non-trivial part of the lax monoidal structure of
F
;
m^{0}:\mathbf{I}\to F(\mathbf{I})
is actually the identity.

5.4.
\mathfrak{TR}_{\with}
-BRTTs coincide with regular functions, via
coherence spaces

We define a streaming setting
\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh}
and its restriction

\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
(with respective underlying categories

\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh}
,
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
) so that

\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
-BRTTs coincide with Alur and D’Antoni’s
notion of single-use-restricted BRTT
[
AD17
]
, which they showed to characterize regular tree functions.
We then show that there are morphisms of streaming settings
\mathfrak{TR}_{\with}\to\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh}\to\mathfrak{TR}_{\with}
and thus establish that
\mathfrak{TR}_{\with}
-BRTTs capture exactly regular tree functions.

Much like
\mathcal{TR}
, the category
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh}
is obtained by applying a generic
construction to
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
, taking weak symmetric multicategories to symmetric affine monoidal
categories. In particular, objects of
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh}
will consist of formal tensor products
of objects of
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
. The main difference is that morphisms of
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh}
will induce
a dependency
relation

D\subseteq I\times J
over indexing sets, rather than a partial function
J\rightharpoonup I
.
This corresponds to a relaxation of the copylessness condition.
However, objects of
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh}
will also be equipped
with a
conflict relation

\incoh
over their indexed sets, and
D
will be required to satisfy a
linearity constraint. Calling
\coh
the dual
coherence relation
such that
x\coh y
is equivalent to
x=y\vee\neg(x\incoh y)
, if we have
(i,j)\in D
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in D
, the linearity constraint enforces

j\coh_{J}j^{\prime}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ i\coh_{I}i^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad i\incoh_{I}i^{\prime}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ j\incoh_{J}j^{\prime}

This corresponds to the
single use restriction
imposed on
BRTTs
[
AD17
, §2.1]
, whose introduction was motivated in

Section

2.2.3
.

{exa}

The BRTT that we gave for the
“conditional swap”
function in
Example
2.2.3
is single-use-restricted according to the above
by taking its two registers (i.e. objects of
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
) to be in
conflict.

But as our choice of notation and vocabulary suggests, this is also related to
the category of (finite)
coherence spaces
, the first denotational model
of linear logic
[
Gir87
]
(predated by a similar semantics for
system F
[
Gir86
]
). As far as we know, this observation is new (the
conflict relation is denoted by
\mathbf{\eta}
in
[
AD17
]
, while
\incoh

comes from the linear logic literature). The coherence semantics of the linear

\lambda
-calculus has been used in particular by Gallot, Lemay and
Salvati
[
GLS20
]
to analyze a top-down tree transducer model
containing linear
\lambda
-terms. Unlike them, we do not use coherence spaces

as a semantics
here; what happens here is much closer the use of a
coherence/conflict relation to handle additive connectives – we will indeed
show a connection with the
\with
-completion – in proof nets,
see
[
Gir96
, Appendix A.1]
and
[
HH16
]
.

{defi}

[see e.g.
[
Gir95
, §2.2.3]
]
A coherence space
I
is a pair
(\|I\|,\coh_{I})
of a set
\|I\|
, called the
web
, and a binary reflexive symmetric
relation
\coh_{I}
over
\|I\|
called the
coherence relation
.
As usual, given a coherence relation
\coh
, we write
\incoh
for the dual defined by
i\incoh i^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow(i=i^{\prime}\vee\neg(i\coh i^{\prime}))
.
Finite coherence spaces are those coherence spaces whose webs are finite.
A
linear map
of coherence spaces
f:I\to J
is a relation
f\subseteq\|I\|\times\|J\|
such that, whenever
(i,j)\in f
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in f
, we have

i\coh_{I}i^{\prime}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ j\coh_{J}j^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad j\incoh_{J}j^{\prime}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ i\incoh_{I}i^{\prime}

Note that these are the
converse implications
of those stated above for BRTTs.

The diagonal
\{(i,i)\mid i\in\|I\|\}
is a linear map
I\to I
and the relational composition of
two linear maps
I\to J\to K
is again a linear map, so that we have
a category
{\sf FinCoh}
whose objects are coherence spaces and morphisms are linear maps.

{\sf FinCoh}
, equipped with the tensorial product

(\|I\|,\coh_{I})\otimes(\|J\|,\coh_{J})=(\|I\|\times\|J\|,\coh_{I}\times\coh_{J})

and dualizing object
(1,1\times 1)
,
is a well-studied
*
-autonomous category with cartesian products and coproducts.
The latter may be defined pointwise as

(\|I\|,\coh_{I})\oplus(\|J\|,\coh_{J})=(\|I\|+\|J\|,\;\coh_{I\oplus J})

where
\coh_{I\oplus J}
is the
smallest
relation such that

\mathsf{in}_{1}(i)\coh_{I\oplus J}\mathsf{in}_{1}(i^{\prime})\ \;\text{when}\ \;i\coh_{I}i^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{in}_{2}(j)\coh_{I\oplus J}\mathsf{in}_{2}(j^{\prime})\ \;\text{when}\ \;j\coh_{J}j^{\prime}

Dualizing an object corresponds to
moving from
\coh
to
\incoh
, i.e.
(\|I\|,\coh_{I})^{\bot}=(\|I\|,\incoh_{I})
, and the product is
I\with J=(I^{\bot}\oplus J^{\bot})^{\bot}
.

With this in mind, we can describe how to turn a multicategory into an affine monoidal category
where monoidal products may be indexed by coherence spaces. The construction has
a vague family resemblance with the

coherence completion of categories
introduced by Hu and Joyal
[
HJ99
]
,
but appears to have quite different properties.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a weak symmetric multicategory. We define
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
to be the category

•

whose objects are pairs
(X,(R_{x})_{x\in\|X\|})
where
X
is a finite coherence space
and
(R_{x})_{x\in\|X\|}
a family of objects of
\mathcal{M}
. We suggestively write
them
\bigodot_{x\in X}R_{x}
.

•

whose morphisms

(f,(\alpha_{y})_{y\in\|Y\|})\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(\bigodot_{x\in X}R_{x},\bigodot_{y\in Y}S_{y}\right)

are pairs consisting of a linear map
f\in\mathsf{Hom}_{{\sf FinCoh}}\left(Y,X\right)
and a family of
multimorphisms
\alpha_{y}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((R_{x})_{x\in f(y)},S_{y}\right)
.

•

whose identities are pairs
(\mathrm{id}_{X},(\mathrm{id}_{R_{x}})_{x\in\|X\|})
.

•

where the composition of

(f,(\alpha_{y})_{y\in\|Y\|})\in\mathsf{Hom}\left(\bigodot_{x\in X}R_{x},\bigodot_{y\in Y}S_{y}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ (g,(\beta_{z})_{z\in\|Z\|})\in\mathsf{Hom}\left(\bigodot_{y\in Y}S_{y},\bigodot_{z\in Z}T_{z}\right)

is
(f\circ g,(\beta_{z}*(\alpha_{y})_{y\in g(z)})_{z\in Z})
.

{defi}

We set
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh}=(\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathrm{coh}}
and

\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
to be its full subcategory consisting of objects

\bigodot_{x\in X}A_{x}
where each
A_{x}
is either empty or a singleton (so
that
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
is isomorphic to

(\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m},\leq 1})_{\mathrm{coh}}
).

Proposition 61
.

BRTTs over the restricted tree streaming setting
\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
compute
exactly the regular tree functions.

Proof 5.6
.

By virtue of being equivalent to Alur and D’Antoni’s notion of
single-use-restricted BRTT
[
AD17
]
. We point the reader to

Appendix

A
for a self-contained definition of those not involving
categories, and leave it as an exercise to formally match those two
descriptions. Although
[
AD17
]
and our
Appendix

A
only consider
BRTTs over
binary
trees, the proof of equivalence between the latter
and regular tree functions goes through
macro tree transducers
(with
regular look-ahead and single use restriction) which are known to compute
regular functions for trees over arbitrary ranked alphabets
[
EM99
]
,
so everything can be made to work out with arbitrary arities in the end.

This being done, the remainder of this section does not depend on
\mathcal{TR}^{\mathrm{m}}
; the arguments apply to any weak symmetric multicategory
\mathcal{M}

and designated object
\Bot\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{M})
.

Accordingly, fix such an
\mathcal{M}
and a
\Bot
for the remainder of this section.
Fix also a set
O
and a map
{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis}:\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((\cdot)_{\varnothing},\Bot\right)\to O
.

Proposition 62
.

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
has a terminal object, given by the unique family over the
empty coherence space, and can be equipped with a symmetric monoidal affine
structure
(\otimes,\top)
where

\left(\bigodot_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\bigodot_{j\in J}B_{j}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigodot_{x\in I\with J}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{i}&\text{if $x=\mathsf{in}_{1}(i)$}\\
B_{j}&\text{if $x=\mathsf{in}_{2}(j)$}\\
\end{array}\right.

and
I\with J
designates the cartesian product in
{\sf FinCoh}
.

Proof 5.7
.

Left to the reader. Strictly speaking, later developments will depend on the
precise structure itself and not merely on its existence, but there is a
single sensible choice of bifunctorial action and structural morphisms making
the above a monoidal product.

Remark 63
.

To start making sense of the use of the cartesian product of
{\sf FinCoh}
,
there is a useful analogy with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}
here.
There is a projection functor
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}\to{\sf PartFinSet}
where
{\sf PartFinSet}

is the category of finite sets and partial functions. The tensorial product of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}

required a coproduct at the level of indices. Here, we have a projection functor
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to{\sf FinCoh}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
and we again use a coproduct at the level of indices (which becomes a product due to the contravariance).

We call
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}
the tree streaming setting based on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}
,
\Bot
and
{\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis})
, and
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
the corresponding tree streaming setting based on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
.

Proposition 64
.

There is a full and faithful strong monoidal functor
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}

extending to a morphism of streaming setting
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
.

Proof 5.8
.

Call
F
this functor, and, for any set
I
, write
\Delta
for the functor

{\sf PartFinSet}\to{\sf FinCoh}
taking a set
I
to the discrete coherence space
\Delta(I)=(I,\{(i,i)\mid i\in I\})
.
Note that we have
\Delta(I)^{\bot}=(I,I\times I)
, which may be regarded as the codiscrete coherence space generated by
I
.
On objects of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}
, we define
F
as

F\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)=\bigodot_{i\in\Delta(I)^{\bot}}A_{i}

For morphisms
(f,(\alpha_{j})_{j\in J})\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}}\left(\bigotimes_{i\in I}A_{i},\bigotimes_{j\in J}B_{j}\right)
, we set

F(f,(\alpha_{j})_{j\in J})=(\{(j,i)\mid j=f(i)\},(\alpha_{j})_{j\in J})

It is rather straightforward to check that
F
is indeed full, faithful and strong monoidal, and the extension to
a morphism
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}\to\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
is immediate.

Proposition 65
.

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
also has cartesian products, which may be defined as

\left(\bigodot_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\with\left(\bigodot_{j\in J}B_{j}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigodot_{x\in I\oplus J}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{i}&\text{if $x=\mathsf{in}_{1}(i)$}\\
B_{j}&\text{if $x=\mathsf{in}_{2}(j)$}\\
\end{array}\right.

(The proof is left to the reader.)
Therefore, we can extend Proposition
64
:

Corollary 66
.

There is a functor
E:(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
that is
full, faithful and lax (but
not
strong) monoidal,
extending to a morphism of streaming settings
(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}\to\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
.

In the following proof and the rest of this section, we write explicitly
\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{coh}}
for the monoidal unit of
\otimes
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}

and
{\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\with}
for the unit in
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
.

Proof 5.9

(Proof idea)
.

The universal property of the free product completion defines
E
as the unique
product-preserving functor extending the functor of Proposition
64
. It remains to equip it with a lax monoidal functor structure. The map
m^{0}:\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to E({\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\with})
is
an obvious isomorphism, while the natural transformation
m^{2}_{A,B}:E(A)\otimes E(B)\to E(A\otimes B)
can be obtained via the canonical map

\left(\bigwith_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\otimes\left(\bigwith_{j\in J}B_{j}\right)\to\bigwith_{(i,j)\in I\times J}A_{i}\otimes B_{j}

in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
(it exists in all monoidal categories with products).

We can now go the other way around.

Lemma 67
.

There is a strong monoidal functor
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
, which extends to
a morphism of streaming settings
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
.

Proof 5.10
.

For a coherence space
(\|X\|,\coh_{X})
, write
\mathrm{Cl}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
the set of

cliques
of
X

\mathrm{Cl}(X)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \{S\in\mathcal{P}(X)\mid\forall x\;y\in S.\;x\coh_{X}y\}

We now define the functor
F:\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
on objects as

F\left(\bigodot_{x\in X}A_{x}\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigwith_{S\in\mathrm{Cl}(X)}\bigotimes_{x\in S}A_{x}

As for morphisms, first recall that a morphism

(R,\alpha)\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\left(\bigodot_{x\in X}A_{x},\bigodot_{y\in Y}B_{y}\right)

consists of a linear map
R\in\mathsf{Hom}_{{\sf FinCoh}}\left(Y,X\right)
and a family

(\alpha_{y})_{y\in\|Y\|}\in\prod_{y\in\|Y\|}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{x})_{(y,x)\in R},B_{y}\right)

We set out to define

F(R,\alpha)\in\mathsf{Hom}_{(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}}\left(F\left(\bigotimes_{x\in S}A_{x}\right),F\left(\bigotimes_{y\in S^{\prime}}B_{y}\right)\right)

recalling that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}}\left(F\left(\bigotimes_{x\in S}A_{x}\right),F\left(\bigotimes_{y\in S^{\prime}}B_{y}\right)\right)\\
\\
\hskip 100.00015pt=\penalty\ \penalty\ \displaystyle\prod_{S^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cl}(Y)}\sum_{S\in\mathrm{Cl}(X)}\sum_{f:S\rightharpoonup S^{\prime}}\prod_{y\in S^{\prime}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}\left((A_{x})_{x\in f^{-1}(x)},B_{y}\right)\end{array}

So fixing
S^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cl}(Y)
and recall that
R
being linear means that we have

(y,x)\in R\wedge(y^{\prime},x)\in R\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcll}y\coh_{Y}y^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&x\coh_{X}x^{\prime}&(1)\\
x=x^{\prime}&\Rightarrow&y\incoh_{Y}y^{\prime}&(2)\\
\end{array}\right.

In particular,
(1)
implies that
\{x\in\|X\|\mid(y,x)\in R\}
is a clique; we take that to be
S
.

(2)
, and the fact that
y\coh_{Y}y^{\prime}\wedge y\incoh_{Y}y^{\prime}\Rightarrow y=y^{\prime}
, imply that
R
determines a
(total) function
S\rightharpoonup S^{\prime}
, which we take to be
f
. Finally, once
y\in S^{\prime}
is fixed, we pick the component
\alpha_{y}

to complete the definition, which makes sense as
f^{-1}(y)=\{x\in S\mid(y,x)\in R\}=\{x\in\|X\|\mid(y,x)\in R)\}
.
This completes the definition of
F(R,\alpha)
; we leave checking functoriality to the reader.

Now, we turn to defining a morphism of streaming setting
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
from
F
.
To this end, we must first equip
F
with a lax monoidal structure, that is to define
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
-maps

m^{0}:{\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{aff}}}_{\with}\to F(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{coh}})\quad\;m^{2}:F\left(\bigodot_{x\in X}A_{x}\right)\otimes F\left(\bigodot_{y\in Y}B_{y}\right)\to F\left(\left[\bigodot_{x\in X}A_{x}\right]\otimes\left[\bigodot_{y\in Y}B_{y}\right]\right)

satisfying the relevant coherence diagrams. We do not check them here, but indicate how to build those two maps.

m^{0}
arises as an obvious isomorphism
F(\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{coh}})\cong\mathbf{I}_{\mathrm{aff}}
.

m^{2}
is also an isomorphism, which may be computed as per Figure
15
.

\displaystyle F\left(\bigodot_{x\in X}A_{x}\right)\otimes F\left(\bigodot_{y\in Y}B_{y}\right)\qquad

\displaystyle\cong\qquad\left(\bigwith_{S\in\mathrm{Cl}(X)}\bigotimes_{x\in S}A_{x}\right)\otimes\left(\bigwith_{S^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cl}(Y)}\bigotimes_{y\in S^{\prime}}B_{y}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\qquad\bigwith\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathrm{Cl}(X)\\
S^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cl}(Y)\end{subarray}}\left(\bigotimes_{x\in X}A_{x}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{y\in Y}B_{y}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\qquad\bigwith_{S\in\mathrm{Cl}(X\with Y)}\bigotimes_{z\in S}C_{z}

\displaystyle\cong\qquad F\left(\bigodot_{z\in X\with Y}C_{z}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\qquad F\left(\left(\bigodot_{x\in X}A_{x}\right)\otimes\left(\bigodot_{y\in Y}B_{y}\right)\right)

Figure 15
.
The main structural natural isomorphism making the functor

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
strong monoidal, writing
A_{x}
for
C_{\mathsf{in}_{1}(x)}

and
B_{y}
for
C_{\mathsf{in}_{2}(y)}
.

Finally, there is a canonical isomorphism
F(\Bot)\cong\Bot
which allows to complete the definition of the morphism
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}\to(\mathfrak{M}_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
.

We thus conclude this section by first specializing the above to the case
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{TR}^{\leq 1}
, and then making a final tangential observation.

Lemma 68
.

There are morphisms of streaming settings
\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\with}\to\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}\to\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\with}
.
In particular,
\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\with}
-BRTTs compute exactly the regular functions.

Remark 69
.

One idea that one could take from Hu and Joyal’s coherence
completion
[
HJ99
]
– but that we do not explore further here – is to
look at objects whose indexing coherence spaces are (up to isomorphism)
generated from singletons by the
\with/\oplus
operations of
{\sf FinCoh}
.
(Those are called
“contractible”
in
[
HJ99
, Section 4]
, and
considering coherence spaces as undirected graphs, this corresponds to the
classical notion of
cograph
in combinatorics.)

In the case of the coherence completion of some category
\mathcal{C}
, the full
subcategory spanned by such objects turns out to be the free completion of

\mathcal{C}
under finite products and coproducts (which differs from our

(-)_{\oplus\with}
in not making ‘
\with
’ distribute over ‘
\oplus
’); this
is formalized as a universal property in
[
HJ99
, Theorem 4.3]
. In the
same vein, we conjecture that the full subcategory of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}

consisting of cograph-indexed objects – that is, of objects that are
generated from those of
\mathcal{M}
by means of the operations
\otimes/\with
in

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{coh}}
– is in some way the
free affine symmetric monoidal
category with products generated by the multicategory
\mathcal{M}
.

5.5.
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}
is monoidal closed

Now, we consider the category
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}
in the context of trees.
Much like with strings, this category is symmetric monoidal monoidal closed with finite products and coproducts,
which makes it an ideal target to compile
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms.
This structure over
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}
is obtained in the same way as for strings:
the monoidal product over
\mathcal{TR}
is defined as distributing over formal sums and
products and the usual products and coproducts are created by the
(-)_{\oplus\with}
completion.
Similarly, monoidal closure can be obtained in a generic way once we show that the objects
coming from
\mathcal{TR}
have internal homsets
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
(echoing Lemma
36
).
This section is mostly dedicated to proving this fact, whose proof relies on decomposing linear trees in
a similar way as in Lemma
57
.

Lemma 70
.

For any two objects
\bf R
and
\bf S
of
\mathcal{TR}
, there is an internal hom
\iota_{\oplus}(\bf R)\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\bf S)
in
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
.

Proof 5.11
.

First, we treat the special case where
{\bf S}=\mathcal{I}(U)
for some finite set
U
.
To make sense of the definition of
\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))
it is helpful to notice that
it will ultimately induce an isomorphism

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}}\left(\top,\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))\right)\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}}\left({\bf R},\mathcal{I}(U)\right)\cong\mathbf{LTree}_{\bf\Gamma}({\bf R}\otimes\mathcal{O}(U))

so, recalling that objects of
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus}
are of the shape
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\bigotimes_{j\in J_{i}}\mathcal{I}(V_{j})
for
V_{j}
being finite sets,
the operational intuition is that one may code trees with “holes with arity” into some bounded finitary data (which we may informally
call a partitioning tree) plus finitely many trees containing holes “without arity”; this bijection is pictured in Figure
16
.

Figure 16
.
Decomposition of a map of
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{TR}(\{a:2,b:1,c:0\})}\left(\{x:3,y:3\},\mathcal{I}(7)\right)
(which is defined as
\mathbf{LTree}_{\{a:2,b:1,c:0\}}(\{x:3,y:3\}\otimes\mathcal{O}(7))
)
as a tuple consisting of a partitioning tree and trees without the letters
x
and
y
.

As with Lemma
57
, we will not use this as our official definition for the internal homset, but rather use the following recursive definition:

•

If
{\bf R}=\top
, set
\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))\penalty\ =\penalty\ \iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))
.

•

Otherwise, define
\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))
as

\bigoplus_{U=V\uplus W}\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(V+1))\otimes\left(\bigoplus_{b\in{\bf\Gamma}}\left({\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)\right)\oplus\bigoplus_{r\in R}\left({\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)\right)\right)\\
\\

where
{\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)
and
{\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)
are auxiliary definitions which correspond to the following situations (recalling that morphisms can be regarded as trees):

–

\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(V+1))\otimes\left({\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)\right)
correspond to morphisms such that there is a
unique
minimal path leading from the root to a node labelled by a letter in
R
, and that letter is
r
.
The second component
{\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)
is meant to include the immediate subtrees of that node while the first
\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(V+1))
contains the tree where a nullary node labeled is inserted instead of that node.
The combination of both these data and
r
allows to recover the original morphism.

–

\bigoplus_{b\in{\bf\Gamma}}\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(V+1))\otimes{\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)
correspond to all the other morphisms.
In such a case there is a topmost node labelled by some letter
b
of
\bf\Gamma
with which has at least two distinct immediate subtrees which have at least one node of
R
each.
Similarly to the first subcase,
{\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)
is intended to include the immediate subtrees of that node labeled by
b
while
\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(V+1))
contains the tree where a nullary node labeled is inserted instead of that node. The combination of these data and
b
allows to recover the original morphism.

Their formal definition is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{*4{>{\displaystyle}l}}&{\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)&=&\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:W\to\mathrm{ar}(b)\\
g:R\to\mathrm{ar}(b)\\
g\text{ nonconstant}\end{subarray}}\bigotimes\limits_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(\Gamma)}\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(f^{-1}(x))\\
\\
&{\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)&=&\bigoplus\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:W\to\mathrm{ar}(r)\\
g:R\setminus\{r\}\to\mathrm{ar}(r)\end{subarray}}\bigotimes\limits_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(r)}\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(f^{-1}(x))\end{array}

Note that the definitions of
\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))
,

{\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)
and
{\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)
mutually depend on one another.
Still this is is well-defined as the definitions of
{\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)
and
{\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)
only require
\iota_{\oplus}({\bf S})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(V))
for
{\bf S}
strictly smaller than
{\bf R}
.

We now describe the associated evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}_{{\bf R},\mathcal{I}(U)}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ (\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U)))\otimes{\bf R}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(U)

also by recursion over
\bf R
.

•

If
{\bf R}=\top
, it is the identity.

•

Otherwise, we need to provide maps

\left(\mathcal{I}(V+1)\otimes\left(\bigoplus_{b\in{\bf\Gamma}}\left({\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)\right)\oplus\bigoplus_{r\in R}\left({\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)\right)\right)\right)\otimes{\bf R}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(U)

for every decomposition
U=V\uplus W
, the intuition being that
\mathcal{I}(V+1)
is a context containing the top of the tree corresponding to the function
we want to apply. Therefore, once we provide a map

\left(\bigoplus_{b\in{\bf\Gamma}}\left({\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)\right)\oplus\bigoplus_{r\in R}\left({\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)\right)\right)\otimes{\bf R}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(W)

we may post-compose it with

\mathcal{I}(V+1)\otimes\mathcal{I}(W)\to\mathcal{I}(V+W)\cong\mathcal{I}(V\uplus W)=\mathcal{I}(U)

to define
\mathrm{ev}_{{\bf R}\multimap\mathcal{I}(U)}
.
Recalling that
\otimes
distributes over
\oplus
, it suffices to provide specialized maps

\mathrm{ev}^{b}_{{\bf R},\mathcal{I}(W)}:{\bf R}\multimap_{b}\mathcal{I}(W)\otimes{\bf R}\to\mathcal{I}(W)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{ev}^{r}_{{\bf R},\mathcal{I}(W)}:{\bf R}\multimap_{r}\mathcal{I}(W)\otimes{\bf R}\to\mathcal{I}(W)

for
b\in\Gamma
and
r\in R
, which we describe now.

–

For
\mathrm{ev}^{b}_{{\bf R},\mathcal{I}(W)}
, it suffices to define a family of
\mathcal{TR}
-maps
indexed by
f:W\to\mathrm{ar}(b)
and
g:R\to\mathrm{ar}(b)
with
g
non-constant

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(\Gamma)}({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))\multimap\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))\right)\otimes{\bf R}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(W)

Recall that
b
can be seen as tree constructor and induces a canonical map

\overline{b}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(W)

Using the induction hypothesis, we have evaluation maps

\left(({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))\multimap\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))\right)\otimes({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(W)

We can then compose
\overline{b}
with the product of those maps over
x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)
and then the isomorphism
{\bf R}\cong\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(b)}{\bf R}\upharpoonright f^{-1}(x)

to conclude the definition of
\mathrm{ev}^{b}_{{\bf R},\mathcal{I}(W)}
.

–

For
\mathrm{ev}^{r}_{{\bf R},\mathcal{I}(W)}
, it suffices to define a family of
\mathcal{TR}
-maps indexed by
f:W\to\mathrm{ar}(r)
and
g:R\setminus\{r\}\to\mathrm{ar}(r)

\left(\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(\Gamma)}({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))\multimap\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))\right)\otimes{\bf R}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{I}(W)

By exploiting the isomorphism
{\bf R}\cong\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{ar}(r))\otimes\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(r)}({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))
and using the inductive hypothesis as in the
previous case, we obtain a map

\left(\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(\Gamma)}({\bf R}\upharpoonright g^{-1}(x))\multimap\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))\right)\otimes{\bf R}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{ar}(r))\otimes\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(r)}\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))

and we may conclude by post-composing by the map

\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{ar}(r))\otimes\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(r)}\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))\to\mathcal{I}(W)

which is induced by the depth-
2
tree whose root
corresponds to the first component, whose children correspond to the successive elements of
\bigotimes_{x\in\mathrm{ar}(r)}\mathcal{I}(f^{-1}(x))
and other leaves are in
W
.

While the definition is a bit wordy, there is then little difficulty in checking that this yields the expected universal property.

\textstyle{\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R}\multimap\mathcal{I}(U))\otimes\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(U))}
\textstyle{A\otimes\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda(h)\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{h}

One needs then to extend the definition for the general case where
\bf S
is not necessarily
\mathcal{I}(U)
; this
is done using a similar approach as for strings, by using a coproduct over partial maps
R\rightharpoonup S
tracking
which letter of the input participates in which letter of the output, and employing the particular case where
there is one letter in the output
27
27

27

This could be factored out as a more general result concerning the existence of internal homsets in categories of the shape
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{aff}}
for multicategories
\mathcal{M}
.

\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}({\bf S})\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{f:R\rightharpoonup S}\bigotimes_{s\in S}\iota_{\oplus}({\bf R}\upharpoonright f^{-1}(s))\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(\mathcal{I}(\mathrm{ar}(s)))

The evaluation function can then be extended and the universal property accordingly lifted to the more general case.

The above lemma tells us that
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}
satisfies the premises
of Theorem
47
, hence:

Theorem 71
.

The category
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}
has cartesian products, coproducts and a
symmetric monoidal
closed
structure.

Remark 72
.

Given the close relationship between the constructions

((-)_{\mathrm{aff}})_{\with}
and
(-)_{\mathrm{coh}}
, it seems plausible that

(\mathcal{TR}_{\mathrm{coh}})_{\oplus}
could be monoidal closed (we know that it has
products, coproducts and a symmetric monoidal structure). We leave this question
to further work.

In other words,
\mathcal{TR}_{\oplus\with}
provides a categorical semantics for
the purely linear fragment of the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus. Concretely, by a suitable
adaptation of Lemma
27
to tree streaming settings, we
finally have:

Corollary 73
.

There is a morphism of streaming settings
\mathfrak{L}\to\mathfrak{TR}_{\oplus\with}
.

5.6.
Preservation properties of finite completions

We have now proven all of the combinatorial results leading to the main theorem for trees.
At this stage, we only need to make explicit a few preservation properties of the completions

(-)_{\oplus}
and
(-)_{\with}
. We only state the minimal requirements that we need to proceed
28
28

28

The following, which would entail these requirements, should hold (but we have
not checked it): the completions
(-)_{\oplus}
and
(-)_{\with}
should behave as pseudo-monads over the
2-category of tree streaming settings, admitting a pseudo-distributive law
((-)_{\oplus})_{\with}\to((-)_{\with})_{\oplus}

giving rise to the pseudo-monad
(-)_{\oplus\with}
.
.

Lemma 74
.

Let
\square\in\{\oplus,\with,\oplus\with\}
and
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{D}
be streaming settings.
Then if there is a morphism
\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{D}
, there is also a morphism
\mathfrak{C}_{\square}\to\mathfrak{C}_{\square}
.

Proof 5.12

(Proof idea)
.

Let us only discuss the case
\square=\oplus
and assume that we have a streaming setting morphism
whose underlying lax monoidal functor is
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
. Then we consider
F_{\oplus}:\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}\to\mathcal{D}_{\oplus}

defined using the universal property of
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}
from the functor
\iota_{\oplus}\circ F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}_{\oplus}

such that

F_{\oplus}\left(\bigoplus_{u\in U}\iota_{\oplus}(C_{u})\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{u\in U}\iota_{\oplus}(F(C_{u}))

so that
F_{\oplus}
inherits a lax monoidal structure from
F
by lifting the relevant maps functorially. For instance,
we have
\iota_{\oplus}(\mathbf{I})\to\iota_{\oplus}(F(\mathbf{I}))=F_{\oplus}(\mathbf{I})
for the unit. We leave checking the coherence diagram, the case of the binary tensor to the reader,
as well as checking that the rest of the structure of morphisms of streaming settings lift accordingly.

Lemma 75
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a streaming setting. Then there are morphisms of streaming settings
\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus\with}\to(\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus})_{\oplus\with}

and
(\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus})_{\oplus\with}\to\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus\with}
.

Proof 5.13

(Proof idea)
.

The first morphism can be obtained using Lemma
74
with the morphism of streaming setting
\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus}

corresponding to
\iota_{\oplus}
. The second morphism can be written as a composite

\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus})_{\oplus\with}\cong((\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus})_{\with})_{\oplus}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathfrak{Dist}_{\mathfrak{C}})_{\oplus}}
\textstyle{((\mathfrak{C}_{\with})_{\oplus})_{\oplus}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{Mu}_{\mathfrak{C}_{\with}}}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{C}_{\with})_{\oplus}\cong\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus\with}}

where
(\mathfrak{Dist}_{\mathfrak{C}})_{\oplus}
is obtained from a morphism
\mathfrak{Dist}_{\mathfrak{C}}:(\mathfrak{C}_{\oplus})_{\with}\to(\mathfrak{C}_{\with})_{\oplus}
by Lemma
74
.

\mathfrak{Dist}_{\mathfrak{C}}
itself is built from a functor
D_{\mathcal{C}}:(\mathcal{C}_{\oplus})_{\with}\to(\mathcal{C}_{\with})_{\oplus}
defined on object following the mantra “products distribute over coproducts”.

D_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\bigwith_{x\in X}\iota_{\with}\left(\bigoplus_{u\in U_{x}}\iota_{\oplus}(C_{x,u})\right)\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{F\in\prod_{x}U_{x}}\iota_{\oplus}\left(\bigwith_{x\in X}\iota_{\with}(C_{x,u})\right)

On the other hand, the functor
M:\mathcal{C}
which is part of
\mathfrak{Mu}_{\mathfrak{C}}
is obtained by using the universal property of
(-)_{\oplus}
so that

M\left(\bigoplus_{u\in U}\iota_{\oplus}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in V}\iota_{\oplus}(C_{u,v})\right)\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \bigoplus_{(u,v)}\iota_{\oplus}(C_{u,v})

5.7.
Proof of the main result on trees

We can now give the proof of Theorem
2
, which can be summarized as the string of equalities

\begin{array}[]{lcl@{\quad}l}\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}&=&\text{single-state $\mathfrak{L}$-BRTTs}&\text{\small by Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:lamlbrtt}}\\
&=&\text{single-state $\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}_{\oplus}$-BRTTs}&(\dagger)\\
&=&\text{$\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}$-BRTTs}&\text{\small by Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:oplus-brtt-conservative}}\\
&=&\text{regular tree functions}&\text{\small by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:brtt-regular}}\end{array}

Note that applying Theorem
55
in the last step requires that objects
of
\mathcal{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
have unitary support, which is easily checked for output alphabets

{\bf\Gamma}
containing at least one letter of arity
\varnothing
.
It remains to justify step
(\dagger)
, i.e.,
that single-state
\mathfrak{L}
-BRTTs and
\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}_{\oplus}
-BRTTs are equi-expressive.
By Lemma
54
, it suffices to have morphisms of streaming settings
both ways
\mathfrak{L}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}_{\oplus}
to conclude. They may be obtained
as the following composites (where we leave implicit the uses of Lemma
74
and some easily inferrable steps).

\textstyle{\mathfrak{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Cor.
73
\textstyle{\mathfrak{TR}_{\oplus\with}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Lem.
75
Cor.
60
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{TR}_{\oplus})_{\oplus\with}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Lem
57
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus})_{\oplus\with}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\oplus\with}\cong\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\mathfrak{TR}^{\leq 1}_{\with})_{\oplus}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Lem.

\textstyle{\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}_{\oplus}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

6.
Conclusion & further work

We have proven that the tree (and string) functions definable at type

\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[A]\multimap\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Gamma}
in the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus
correspond exactly to regular functions. To prove the non-trivial left-to-right
inclusion, we used a syntactic lemma for
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms
allowing to compile those terms into
a kind of transducer model using purely linear
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms as its memory (
\mathfrak{L}
-BRTTs).
We then showed in a principled way
that those transducers are equivalent to the more standard single-use-restricted
Bottom-up Ranked Tree Transducers
(expressed as
\mathfrak{TR}^{\incoh,\leq 1}
-BRTTs in our framework) which capture regular tree functions.
Along the way, we revisited a few results on string
transducers under the light of our categorical framework, exhibiting some points
of convergence with our semantics for
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms.

There are a number of ways one could plan on expanding this work. We list some
of the most relevant problems, regarding the definable
functions between strings and trees in various
\lambda
-calculi, that we expect could be tackled using similar
methods or minor variations of our setting.

Removing the additive connectives

\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
features the additive connectives
\oplus
and
\with
(as well as the relevant
units
0
and
\top
). A natural question is whether all regular functions are
still encodable if we remove access to those connectives and use the
\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }
-calculus, an
affine

version of
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
without those connectives; for strings, this is first half of
[
NP20
, Claim 6.2]
.

In a sense, we went further than that in showing that
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-definable functions are regular,
since it can be checked that all
\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }
-definable string-to-string functions are also
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-definable.
However, we did not give evidence for the converse direction, as our encoding of states of BRTTs use the
additive connectives. We believe this can be remedied by leveraging our previous
work on encoding sequential transducers
[
NP20
, Sections 4-5]
(that
relies on the highly non-trivial Krohn-Rhodes theorem).

As for functions taking trees as input, we suspect that the

\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }
-calculus is
strictly less expressive
than copyless BRTTs
(
\mathfrak{TR}
-BRTTs) – in fact, that
\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }
-terms are
even unable to recognize all regular tree
languages
. We expect that this can be shown
by interpreting purely affine
\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }
-terms into (a variant of) the geometry of
interaction semantics mentioned in Example
18
.
Our intent is to deduce from this that
\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }
-definable languages can be recognized by
tree-walking
automata
– which are indeed a natural extension to trees of the two-way
automata from Example
18
– and some regular tree languages are known to be unrecognizable by
such devices
[
BC08
]
.

Anecdotally, we show in
Appendix

F
that removing the additive
disjunction ‘
\oplus
’ from the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus while keeping the additive
conjunction ‘
\with
’ does not affect the definability of tree functions. While
this is a routine application of a continuation-passing-style transformation,
the nice thing is that it can be entirely carried out at the level of streaming
settings.

Dropping commutativity

The second half of
[
NP20
, Claim 6.2]
raised the issue of definable string-to-string
transductions in the
\lambda\wp
-calculus, the restriction of the
\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }
-calculus to a
non-commutative
multiplicative structure.
By analogy with the main result of
[
NP20
]
, which was that

\lambda\wp
-definable languages are star-free (i.e. first-order definable) while
\lambda\kern-0.50003pt\mbox{ \enskip{1 0 0.4 1} \hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{\text{a}}} }

defines regular
languages, the natural guess is that they correspond to
first-order regular
functions
. We expect non-commutative typing to translate into a

planarity
condition in the above-mentioned category of diagrams (close to a free (non-symmetric) compact-closed category
[
KL80
]
).

The situation with trees looks more delicate, although trying to compare the
expressiveness of the

\lambda\wp
-calculus with the functional combinators described in
[
BD20
]
might be a good starting point.

Duplicating the input

Going to higher complexities, but without escalating to the towers of
exponentials definable in the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus,
one natural question is: how expressive are
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms of type
\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]\to\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Gamma}
, for purely linear
\kappa
? We expect that this problem
could be tackled by reusing our semantic
interpretation of purely linear
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms together with a suitable
refinement of Lemma
25
. Concerning the case of strings,
our current conjecture is that the expressible functions have polynomial growth
and form a strict subclass of
polyregular
functions

[
Boj18
,
BKL19
]
.

Semantic interpretation of exponentials

One frustrating aspect of our approach is that we need to rely on the normalization of
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms
and a special-purpose syntactic lemma to dissect the term under consideration and extract the relevant purely
linear subterms before being able to go to semantic interpretations and more
high-level arguments.
The reason behind this is that our semantics does not interpret the non-linear arrow
‘
\to
’ or, equivalently, a version of the exponential modality ‘
\oc
’ of linear logic.
It might be more pleasant to have more general semantic settings with those features that still allow
us to show our characterizations. Furthermore, such a setting would be
necessary
if we hope to
tackle the question of the expressiveness of functions
\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[A]\to\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}

for general
A
using semantic tools (this last variant of the problem is equivalent to the setting
of the simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus without linearity constraints).

Appendix A
Alur and D’Antoni’s Bottom-Up Ranked Tree Transducers

We give here a self-contained definition of the notion of BRTT corresponding to
regular tree functions, as they were designed in
[
AD17
]
. Since the
paper
[
AD17
]
is mainly concerned with transducers over unranked trees, the
information concerning the definition of BRTTs is spread over its sections 2.1,
3.7 and 3.8. We restrict here
to binary trees (as in
[
AD17
, Sections 3.7 and 3.8]
) and avoid using
multicategories.

{defi}

[
[
AD17
, p. 31:36]
]
The set
\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
of
binary trees
over the alphabet
\Sigma
,
and the set
\partial\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
of
one-hole binary trees
are generated
by the respective grammars

T,U::=\lceil\cdot\rceil\mid a\lceil T,U\rceil\quad(a\in\Sigma)\qquad\qquad T^{\prime},U^{\prime}::=\square\mid a\lceil T^{\prime},U\rceil\mid a\lceil T,U^{\prime}\rceil\quad(a\in\Sigma)

That is,
\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
consists of binary trees whose leaves are all
equal to
\langle\rangle
and whose nodes are labeled with letters in

\Sigma
. As for
\partial\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
, it contains trees with exactly one leaf
labeled
\square
instead of
\lceil\cdot\rceil
. This
“hole”

\square
is
intended to be substituted by a tree: for
T^{\prime}\in\partial\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
and
U\in\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
,
T^{\prime}[U]
denotes
T^{\prime}
where
\square
has been replaced
by
U
.

{defi}
[
[
AD17
, p. 31:40]
]
The
binary tree expressions
(
E,F
below, forming the set

\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma,V,V^{\prime})
) and
one-hole binary tree expressions
(
E^{\prime},F^{\prime}

below, forming the set
\mathrm{Expr}\partial\mathrm{BT}(\Sigma,V,V^{\prime})
) over the variable sets
V
and

V^{\prime}
are generated by the grammar (with
x\in V
,
x^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
and
a\in\Sigma
)

E,F::=\lceil\cdot\rceil\mid x\mid a\lceil E,F\rceil\mid E^{\prime}[F]\qquad E^{\prime},F^{\prime}:=\square\mid x^{\prime}\mid a\lceil E^{\prime},F\rceil\mid a\lceil E,F^{\prime}\rceil\mid E^{\prime}[F^{\prime}]

Given
\rho:V\to\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
and
\rho^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\to\partial\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
,
one defines
E(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\in\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
for
E\in\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma)

and
E^{\prime}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\in\partial\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)
for
E\in\mathrm{Expr}\partial\mathrm{BT}(\Sigma)
in the
obvious way.

{defi}

[
[
AD17
, §3.7]
]
Let us fix an input alphabet
\Gamma
and output alphabet
\Sigma
. The set of

register assignments
over two disjoint sets
R,R^{\prime}
, whose elements are
called
registers
, is

\mathcal{A}(\Sigma,R,R^{\prime})=\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma,R_{\triangleleft\triangleright},R^{\prime}_{\triangleleft\triangleright})^{R}\times\mathrm{Expr}\partial\mathrm{BT}(\Sigma,R_{\triangleleft\triangleright},R^{\prime}_{\triangleleft\triangleright})^{R^{\prime}}\quad\text{where}\ R_{\triangleleft\triangleright}=R\times\{\triangleleft,\triangleright\}

A
register tree transducer
consists of a
finite
set
Q
of

states
with an
initial state

q_{I}\in Q
, two disjoint

finite
sets
R,R^{\prime}
of
registers
, a
transition function

\delta:Q\times Q\times\Gamma\to Q\times\mathcal{A}(\Sigma,R,R^{\prime})
and an

output function

F:Q\to\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma,R,R^{\prime})
. To each tree
T\in\mathrm{BinTree}(\Gamma)
, it associates inductively a
configuration

\mathrm{Conf}(T)\in Q\times\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)^{R}\times\partial\mathrm{BinTree}(\Sigma)^{R^{\prime}}
:

•

The base case is
\mathrm{Conf}(\lceil\cdot\rceil)=(q_{I},(r\mapsto\lceil\cdot\rceil),(r^{\prime}\mapsto\square))
.

•

When
\mathrm{Conf}(T)=(q_{\triangleleft},\rho_{\triangleleft},\rho^{\prime}_{\triangleleft})
,
\mathrm{Conf}(U)=(q_{\triangleright},\rho_{\triangleright},\rho^{\prime}_{\triangleright})
and
\delta(c,q_{\triangleleft},q_{\triangleright})=(q,(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}))
, we set
\mathrm{Conf}(c\lceil T,U\rceil)=(q,(r\mapsto\varepsilon(r)(\rho,\rho^{\prime})),(r^{\prime}\mapsto\varepsilon^{\prime}(r^{\prime})(\rho,\rho^{\prime})))
where
\rho(r,d)=\rho_{d}(r)
for
(r,d)\in R\times\{\triangleleft,\triangleright\}
and similarly for
\rho^{\prime}
.

The function defined by the transducer is
T\in\mathrm{BinTree}(\Gamma)\mapsto F(q_{\mathrm{fin}}(T))(\rho_{\mathrm{fin}}(T),\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fin}}(T))
where

(q_{\mathrm{fin}}(T),\rho_{\mathrm{fin}}(T),\rho^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fin}}(T))=\mathrm{Conf}(T)
(recall that
F
is the output function).

Figure 17.
First few steps of the run of the bottom-up ranked tree transducer of
Figure
A
over a tree whose alphabet of node labels is
\Sigma=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet},{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bullet}\}\cong\{a,b\}
.

The configuration at each subtree is represented
by two boxes; the top (resp. bottom) box displays the contents of
r_{1}

(resp.
r_{2}
). (The single state is omitted from the visual representation of
the configuration.)

{exa}

[illustrated by Figure
17
]

Let us consider a transducer over the alphabets
\Gamma=\Sigma=\{a,b\}
,
with a single state (
|Q|=1
) and two registers, both tree-valued (so
R=\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
and
R^{\prime}=\varnothing
). This simplifies the transition function

\delta
into a function
\Gamma\to\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma,R_{\triangleleft\triangleright},\varnothing)^{R}
– equivalently, we will consider

\delta:\Gamma\times R\to\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma,R_{\triangleleft\triangleright},\varnothing)
.

We take
\delta(c,r_{1})=c\lceil(r_{1})_{\triangleleft},(r_{1})_{\triangleright}\rceil
and
\delta(c,r_{2})=c\lceil(r_{2})_{\triangleright},(r_{2})_{\triangleleft}\rceil
for
c\in\{a,b\}
, where
r_{\triangleleft}
is a
notation for
(r,\triangleleft)\in R_{\triangleleft\triangleright}=R\times\{\triangleleft,\triangleright\}
. If we write

\hat{r}_{i}(T)
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) for the contents of the register
r_{i}
at the
end of a run of the transducer on
T\in\mathtt{BT}(\Gamma)
, then this
\delta

translates into:

\hat{r}_{1}(c\lceil T,U\rceil)=c\lceil\hat{r}_{1}(T),\hat{r}_{1}(U)\rceil\qquad\hat{r}_{2}(c\lceil T,U\rceil)=c\lceil\hat{r}_{2}(U),\hat{r}_{2}(T)\rceil\qquad(c\in\{a,b\})

And the initial condition is
\hat{r}_{1}(\lceil\cdot\rceil)=\hat{r}_{2}(\lceil\cdot\rceil)=\lceil\cdot\rceil
. Therefore
\hat{r}_{1}(T)=T
and
\hat{r}_{2}(T)
is
T

“mirrored”
by exchanging left and right; let us write
\hat{r}_{2}(T)=\mathtt{reverse}(T)
.

The output function
F
is also simplified into an expression in

\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma,R,\varnothing)
. By taking
F=a\lceil r_{1},r_{2}\rceil
, we define
a transducer computing the regular function
T\mapsto a\lceil T,\mathtt{reverse}(T)\rceil
.

To characterize regular functions, a condition must be imposed
29
29

29

Without
this restriction, one could have outputs of exponential size, whereas regular
functions have linearly bounded output. Filiot and
Reynier
[
FR21
]
have shown that the analogous unrestricted model
for strings corresponds to HDT0L systems; we are not aware of any similar
result on trees.
on the register assignments: the
single use
restriction
. It is not intrinsic and depends on an additional piece of data,
namely a
conflict relation
between the registers.

{defi}
[
[
AD17
, §2.1]
]

A
conflict relation
is a binary reflexive and symmetric relation.

An expression
E\in\mathrm{ExprBT}(\Sigma,V,V^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{Expr}\partial\mathrm{BT}(\Sigma,V,V^{\prime})
is said
to be
consistent with
a conflict relation
\incoh
over
V\cup V^{\prime}

when each variable in
V\cup V^{\prime}
appears at most once in
E
, and for all

x,y\in V\cup V^{\prime}
, if
x\neq y
and
x\incoh y
, then
E
does not contain
both
x
and
y
.

A register assignment
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}(\Sigma,R,R^{\prime})
is

single use restricted
with respect to a conflict relation
\incoh
over

R\cup R^{\prime}
when:

•

all
\varepsilon(r)
for
r\in R
and all
\varepsilon^{\prime}(r^{\prime})
for
r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
are consistent with
\incoh
;

•

if
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in R\cup R^{\prime}
,
x_{1}\incoh x_{2}
and, for some
d\in\{\triangleleft,\triangleright\}
,
(x_{1},d)
appears in
30
30

30

By
\varepsilon\cup\varepsilon^{\prime}
we mean the map on the
disjoint
union
R\cup R^{\prime}

induced in the obvious way by
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

(\varepsilon\cup\varepsilon^{\prime})(y_{1})
and
(x_{2},d)
appears in

(\varepsilon\cup\varepsilon^{\prime})(y_{2})
, then
y_{1}\incoh y_{2}
(note that
this includes the case
x_{1}=x_{2}
).

{defi}

A
bottom-up ranked tree transducer (BRTT)
is a register tree transducer

(Q,q_{I},R,R^{\prime},\delta,F)
endowed with a conflict relation
\incoh
on
R\cup R^{\prime}
, such that
F(q)
is consistent with
\incoh
and all register assignments
in the image of
\delta
are single use restricted w.r.t.
\incoh
.

A
regular tree function
is a function computed by a BRTT.

When the conflict relation is trivial (i.e. coincides with equality), we say
that the BRTT is
copyless
. We also say that a register tree transducer
is copyless if it becomes a BRTT when endowed with a trivial conflict
relation.

Appendix B
Normalization of the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus

This section is devoted to proving that the
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-calculus is strongly
normalizing. What it means is that any
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-term
t
admitting a typing
derivation
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash t:A
can be shown to be

\beta\eta
-equivalent to a
normal
term
u
.
The notion of normal (
\mathrm{NF}
) and
neutral
(
\mathrm{NE}
) are
defined via the typing system presented in Figure
20
.
The intuition is that a normal term cannot be
\beta
-reduced further, and
that neutral terms substituted in normal terms produce terms that stay normal.

The purpose of this section is to show that
any
typed term
t
can
be turned into a normal term
t^{\prime}
of the same type via a sequence of
\beta
-reductions.
Because
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
features
positive
type constructors
\otimes
and
\oplus
,
the reality is not quite so straightforward: it is not sufficient to
\beta
-reduce
a term to reach a normal form. In certain circumstances, one must additionally use

\eta
-conversion to make certain
\beta
-redexes appear and proceed with the
computation of normal terms.
This difficulty is rather well-known in the context of
\lambda
-calculus
extended with coproducts
[
Lin07
,
ADHS01
,
Sch16
]
.
However we are not aware of a text treating exactly
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
(e.g., incorporating
additives, a native
\otimes
and units), so we include a proof using reducibility
candidates along the lines of
[
RR97
, Appendix A.1]
.

To describe said reducibility candidates, we first need to give an oriented version
of
\beta\eta
-equality.
The
\beta
-reduction relation
\to_{\beta}
is obtained by closing the relations
given in Figure
18
by congruence. We write
\to_{\beta}^{*}
for the
reflexive transitive closure relation. Much like with
=_{\beta}
, we assume that
terms related by
\to_{\beta}
have the same type in the same context.

\begin{array}[]{l !\; c !{\;\;} c}\text{$\beta$-redexes}&(\lambda x.t)\;u\penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ t[u/x]&(\lambda^{\oc}x.t)\;u\penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ t[u/x]\\
&\pi_{1}(\left\langle t,u\right\rangle)\penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ t&\pi_{2}(\left\langle t,u\right\rangle)\penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ u\\
&{\mathsf{case}}(\mathsf{in}_{1}(t),x.u,x.v)\penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ u[t/x]&{\mathsf{case}}(\mathsf{in}_{2}(t),x.u,x.v)\penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ v[t/x]\\
&{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={t\otimes u}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{v}\penalty\ \penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ \penalty\ v[t/x][u/y]&{\mathsf{let}}\;{()}={()}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{t}\penalty\ \to_{\beta}\penalty\ t\\
\\
\end{array}

Figure 18.
\beta
-redexes.

As it is not the case that every typable term
\beta
-reduces
to a normal form, we need to describe another set of reduction rules which involve
=_{\eta}
.
Those
extrusion rules
are listed in Figure
19
; we also write

\to_{\varepsilon}
for the congruence closure of the relation described there.
While the number of cases is daunting, it should be remarked that these rules are
obtained mechanically by considering the nesting of an eliminator for a positive type
(i.e., the
{\mathsf{let}}\;{\cdot}={\cdot}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{\cdot}
constructions (
\otimes,\mathbf{I}
),
{\mathsf{case}}
(
\oplus
) and
{\mathsf{abort}}
(
0
))
within another eliminator (the aforementioned constructions plus function application (
\to,\multimap
) and
projections
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
(
\with
)). For a more careful discussion of (a subset) of these rules, we point
the reader to
[
Sch16
, Section 3.3]
.
We write
\to_{\varepsilon}^{*}
for the reflexive transitive closure of
\to_{\varepsilon}
,
\to_{\beta\varepsilon}
for the
union of
\to_{\varepsilon}
and
\to_{\beta}
and
\to_{\varepsilon}
, and
\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}
for its reflexive transitive
closure. With these notations, we can state the finer version of the normalization theorem for
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
.

Theorem 76
.

For every term term
t
such that
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash t:\tau
, there exists
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}t^{\prime}
and

\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\tau
.

Before embarking on the definitions of the reducibility candidates and the proof of
Theorem
76
itself, we first make a couple of observations
relating
\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}
and
=_{\beta\eta}
.

Lemma 77
.

Suppose that we terms
t
and
t^{\prime}
with matching types and
that
t\to_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
. Then, we have
t=_{\beta\eta}t^{\prime}
.
Furthermore if
t
is normal, so is
t^{\prime}
. Similarly, if
t
is neutral,
so is
t^{\prime}
.

Lemma 78
.

If
t
is normal, then there is no
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to_{\beta}t^{\prime}
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\footnotesize\text{Nested $\otimes/\mathbf{I}$ eliminator}\\

Both of these Lemmas are proved by straightforward induction on the
relations
\to_{\beta}
and
\to_{\beta\varepsilon}
.

Another crucial ingredient is the confluence of the reduction relation
\to_{\beta\varepsilon}
(or Church-Rosser property).
Alas, this does not hold for syntactic equality (up to
\alpha
-equivalence. However, it holds up to
commuting conversions
,
an equivalence relation
\approx_{c}
inductively defined by the clauses in Figure
21
and closure under congruence.
We merely state the confluence property that we will use.

Theorem 79
.

If we have
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}u
and
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}v
, then there exists
u^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}
such that

u\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}u^{\prime}
,
v\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}v^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}\approx_{c}v^{\prime}
.

A first observation is these are compatible with
=_{\beta\eta}
and
that neutral and normal term are preserved by commutative conversions.

Lemma 80
.

If
t\approx_{c}t^{\prime}
, then
t=_{\beta\eta}t^{\prime}
.

Lemma 81
.

If
t
is neutral (resp. normal) and
t\approx_{c}t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}
is also neutral (resp. normal).

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\footnotesize{\mathsf{let}}\;{q}={u}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{{\mathsf{let}}\;{p}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{v}}&\approx_{c}&{\mathsf{let}}\;{p}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{{\mathsf{let}}\;{q}={u}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{v}}\\
{\mathsf{let}}\;{p}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{{\mathsf{abort}}(u)}&\approx_{c}&{\mathsf{abort}}({\mathsf{let}}\;{p}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u})\\
{\mathsf{let}}\;{p}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{{\mathsf{case}}(u,x.v,y.w)}&\approx_{c}&{\mathsf{case}}(u,x.{\mathsf{let}}\;{p}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{v},y.{\mathsf{let}}\;{p}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{w})\\
{\mathsf{case}}(u,x.{\mathsf{abort}}(t),y.{\mathsf{abort}}(v))&\approx_{c}&{\mathsf{abort}}({\mathsf{case}}(u,x.t,y.v))\\
\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.{\mathsf{case}}(u,x^{\prime}.v,y^{\prime}.w),y.{\mathsf{case}}(u,x^{\prime}.v^{\prime},y^{\prime}.w^{\prime}))\\
\approx_{c}\\
{\mathsf{case}}(u,x^{\prime}.{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.v,y.v^{\prime}),y^{\prime}.{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.w,y.w^{\prime}))\\
\end{array}\\
\end{array}
