Commutative conversions
\approx_{c}
(
p,q
are patterns
()
or
z\otimes z^{\prime}
and both sides are assumed to be well-scoped).

We can now turn to the definition of the reducibility candidates,
where we write
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}\approx_{c}t^{\prime}
when there
is some
t^{\prime\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime\prime}
and
t^{\prime\prime}\approx_{c}t^{\prime}

hold.

{defi}

Define a judgment
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau
by induction over
the type
\tau
as follows:

•

for
\tau=\mathbbl{o},\mathbf{I},0
or
\top
, we have
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau
if and only if there is

t^{\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t^{\prime}:\tau

•

\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau\multimap\sigma
holds if and only if

–

there is
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\tau\multimap\sigma

–

for every
u,\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\models u:\tau
, we have

\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\models t\;u:\sigma

•

\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau\to\sigma
holds if and only if

–

there is
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\tau\to\sigma

–

for every
u
such that
\Psi;\;\cdot\models u:\tau
, we have

\Psi;\;\Delta\models t\;u:\sigma

•

\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau\otimes\sigma
holds if and only if

–

there is
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\tau\otimes\sigma

–

if there are
t_{1},t_{2}
such that and
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t_{1}\otimes t_{2}
, then
there are
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
such that
\Delta=\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
and

\Psi;\;\Delta_{1}\models t_{1}:\tau\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta_{2}\models t_{2}:\sigma

•

\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau\with\sigma
holds if and only if

–

there is
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\tau\with\sigma

–

\Psi;\;\Delta\models\pi_{1}(t):\tau

–

\Psi;\;\Delta\models\pi_{2}(t):\sigma

•

\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau\oplus\sigma
holds if and only if

–

there is
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\tau\oplus\sigma

–

if there is
u
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}\approx_{c}\mathsf{in}_{1}(u)
, then
\Psi;\;\Delta\models u:\tau

–

if there is
v
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}\approx_{c}\mathsf{in}_{2}(v)
, then
\Psi;\;\Delta\models v:\sigma

The set of terms
t
such that
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau
constitutes our set of reducibility candidates
at type
\tau
in the context
\Psi;\;\Delta
. They are defined in such a way that if

\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau
, then there is
t^{\prime}
such that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\tau
. We shall be able to conclude this section if we show an
adequacy lemma
stating that
every typable term lies in a reducibility candidate.
Before doing that, we first need a couple of stability properties: closure under anti-reduction, and the
fact that every neutral term lies in a reducibility candidate.

Lemma 82
.

If
t
is a neutral term, then
t
cannot be
\beta\eta
-equivalent to one of the following

\lambda x.u\qquad\lambda^{\oc}x.u\qquad(u,v)\qquad u\otimes v\qquad\langle\rangle\qquad()\qquad\mathsf{in}_{1}(u)\qquad\mathsf{in}_{2}(u)

Proof B.1
.

Trivial case-analysis.

Theorem 83
.

Suppose that
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t^{\prime}:\tau
.
Then the following hold:

•

There exists
t^{\prime\prime}
such that
t^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime\prime}:\tau
.

•

If we have
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
, then we also have
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau
.

•

If
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau
, then
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\tau
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\approx_{c}t^{\prime\prime}
, then
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t^{\prime\prime}:\tau
.

•

If
t^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime\prime}
, then we also have
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t^{\prime\prime}:\tau
.

Proof B.2
.

The first point can be proven via an easy case analysis on
\tau
that we skip.
The second point we may prove by induction over
\tau
; let us sketch a few representative cases:

•

If
\tau
is
\mathbbl{o}
,
0
,
\mathbf{I}
or
\top
, this is immediate.

•

Suppose that
\tau\penalty\ =\penalty\ \sigma\multimap\kappa
and that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
. By definition of
\models
at
\tau\multimap\kappa
, there is some normal
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime\prime}
, so we also have
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime\prime}
by transitivity. Now suppose that we are given some

u
and
\Delta^{\prime}
such that
\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\models u:\tau
. By definition, we have
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\models t^{\prime}\;u:\kappa
.
Using the induction hypothesis at
\kappa
and the fact that
t\;u\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}\;u
, we thus have that
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\models t\;u:\kappa
.
We can thus conclude that
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\kappa
.

•

Suppose that
\tau\penalty\ =\penalty\ \sigma\oplus\kappa
and that
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
. By definition of
\models
, there is some normal
t^{\prime\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime\prime}
, so we also have
t\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime\prime}
by transitivity.
Now if we have
u
(resp.
v
) such that
t\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}\approx_{c}\mathsf{in}_{1}(u)
(resp.
\mathsf{in}_{2}(v)
), then, thanks to confluence (Theorem
79
) we also have
t^{\prime}\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}\approx_{c}\mathsf{in}_{1}(u)
(resp.
\mathsf{in}_{2}(v)
),
so we have
\Psi;\;\Delta\models u:\sigma
(resp.
\Psi;\;\Delta\models u:\kappa
) by definition.
Therefore, we may conclude that
\Psi;\;\Delta\models t:\sigma\oplus\kappa
.

The third point is also proved via a straightforward induction over
\tau
by leveraging Lemma
82
.
The last two points follow from induction over
\tau
combined with Theorem
79
and Lemma
81
.

Corollary 84
.

If
x
is a variable of type
\tau
in either
\Psi
or
\Delta
, we have
\Psi;\;\Delta\models x:\tau
.

Proof B.3
.

Immediate as variables are neutral.

Lemma 85
.

Suppose that we have
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\otimes\sigma
and
\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime},x:\tau,y:\sigma\models u:\kappa
.
Then we have
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\models{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\kappa
.

Similarly, if
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\oplus\sigma
,

\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime},x:\tau\models u:\kappa
and

\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime},y:\sigma\models u^{\prime}:\kappa
, we have
\Psi;\;\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\models{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.u,y.u^{\prime}):\kappa
.

Finally, if
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\mathbf{I}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\models u:\kappa
, we also have
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\models{\mathsf{let}}\;{()}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\kappa
.

Proof B.4
.

By induction over
\kappa
.

Theorem 86

(Adequacy)
.

Suppose that we have a non-linear context
\Psi\penalty\ =\penalty\ x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\;\ldots,\;x_{k}:\sigma_{k}
, a linear context

\Delta\penalty\ =\penalty\ a_{1}:\tau_{1},\ldots,\;a_{n}:\tau_{n}
and a term
v
such that
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash v:\kappa
for some type
\kappa
.
Further, assume that
\Psi^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{\prime}_{n}
and terms
t_{1},\ldots t_{k},u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}
such that

\Psi^{\prime};\;\cdot\models t_{i}:\sigma_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{j}\models u_{j}:\tau_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq n
.
Then we have

\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\;\Delta^{\prime}_{n}\models v[t_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,t_{k}/x_{k},u_{1}/a_{1},\ldots,u_{n}/a_{n}]:\kappa

Proof B.5
.

The proof goes by induction over the typing derivation
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash v:\kappa
; we sketch a few representative subcases below.
To keep notations short, we write
\gamma
(respectively
\delta
) instead of the sequence of assignments
t_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,t_{k}/x_{k}
(respectively
u_{1}/a_{1},\ldots,u_{n}/a_{n}
) and

\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\;\Delta^{\prime}_{n}
.

•

If the last rule used in an axiom, the conclusion is immediate.

•

If the last rule used is a linear function application

\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta_{1}\vdash v:\kappa\multimap\kappa^{\prime}\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta_{2}\vdash v^{\prime}:\kappa}{\Psi;\;\Delta_{1},\;\Delta_{2}\vdash v\;v^{\prime}:\kappa^{\prime}}

with
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{1},\;\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{2}
the obvious decomposition of
\delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
,
the induction hypothesis yields

\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{1}\models v[\gamma,\delta_{1}]:\kappa\multimap\kappa^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{2}\models v^{\prime}[\gamma,\delta_{2}]:\kappa

By definition of
\models
for type
\kappa\multimap\kappa^{\prime}
, we thus have
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}\models v[\gamma,\delta_{1}]\;v^{\prime}[\gamma,\delta_{2}]:\kappa^{\prime}
, so we may conclude.

•

The case of non-linear function application is entirely analogous.

•

If the last rule is the typing of a linear
\lambda
-abstraction

\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;c:\kappa\vdash v:\kappa^{\prime}}{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash\lambda c.v:\kappa\multimap\kappa^{\prime}}

by the inductive hypothesis, we have
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vdash v[\gamma,\delta,v^{\prime}/c]:\kappa^{\prime}
for any
v^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
such that
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\models v^{\prime}:\kappa
.
We can prove the conjunct defining
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}\models(\lambda c.v)[\gamma,\delta]:\kappa\multimap\kappa^{\prime}
as follows:

–

First, by taking
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=c:\kappa
(Corollary
84
), we obtain that there exists some
v^{\prime\prime}
such that
v[\gamma,\delta]\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}v^{\prime\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;c:\kappa\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}v^{\prime\prime}:\kappa^{\prime}
.
Therefore we have
\lambda c.v[\gamma,\delta]\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}\lambda c.v^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}\lambda c.v^{\prime}:\kappa\multimap\kappa^{\prime}
.

–

Then, assume we have some
v^{\prime}
and
\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\models v^{\prime}:\kappa
, so that we have
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime\prime}\vdash v[\gamma,\delta,v^{\prime}/c]:\kappa^{\prime}
by the inductive hypothesis.
Because

(\lambda c.v)[\gamma,\delta]\;v^{\prime}=(\lambda c.v[\gamma,\delta])\;v^{\prime}\to_{\beta}v[\gamma,\delta,v^{\prime}/c]

we may apply Theorem
83
to conclude that
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime},\;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\models(\lambda c.v)[\gamma,\delta]\;v^{\prime}:\kappa^{\prime}

•

The case of the non-linear
\lambda
-abstraction for
\to
is similar.

•

If the last rule applied is an introduction of
\otimes
,

\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta_{1}\vdash t:\tau\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta_{2}\vdash u:\sigma}{\Psi;\;\Delta_{1},\;\Delta_{2}\vdash t\otimes u:\tau\otimes\sigma}

call
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
the splitting of
\delta
according to the decomposition
\Delta=\Delta_{1},\;\Delta_{2}
.
The induction hypothesis yields

\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\models t[\gamma,\delta_{1}]:\tau\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{2}\models u[\gamma,\delta_{2}]:\sigma

By definition it means that we have normal terms
t^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}
such that
t[\gamma,\delta_{1}]\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t^{\prime}
and
u[\gamma,\delta_{2}]\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}u^{\prime}
,
so
(t\otimes u)[\gamma,\delta]\to^{*}_{\beta\varepsilon}t\otimes u^{\prime}
.
Now suppose that we have
(t\otimes u)[\gamma,\delta]\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}\approx_{c}t^{\prime\prime}\otimes u^{\prime\prime}
. It is not difficult to check (by induction over the length
of the reductions
\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}
and derivation of
\approx_{c}
) that we have
t[\gamma,\delta_{1}]\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}\approx_{c}t^{\prime\prime}
and
u[\gamma,\delta_{2}]\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}\approx_{c}u^{\prime\prime}
.
So by Theorem
83
, we have that
\Psi;\;\Delta_{1}\models t^{\prime\prime}:\tau
and
\Psi;\;\Delta_{2}\models u^{\prime\prime}:\sigma
,so we may conclude.

•

If the last rule applied is an elimination of
\otimes

\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta_{1}\vdash u:\tau\otimes\sigma\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta_{2},x:\tau,y:\sigma\vdash t:\kappa}{\Psi;\;\Delta_{1},\;\Delta_{2}\vdash{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={u}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{t}:\kappa}

with
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
the obvious decomposition of
\delta
along
\Delta_{1},\;\Delta_{2}
,
the induction hypothesis applied to the first premise yields

\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\models u[\gamma,\delta_{1}]:\tau\otimes\sigma
.
In particular, this means we have
u[\gamma,\delta_{1}]\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}u^{\prime}
such that

\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u^{\prime}:\tau\otimes\sigma
. By Theorem
83
,
it suffices to show that
{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={u^{\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{t[\gamma,\delta_{2}]}\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}v

such that
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{1},\;\Delta^{\prime}_{2}\models v:\kappa
to conclude.
We do so by going by induction over the judgment
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u^{\prime}:\tau\otimes\sigma
.

–

If we have
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u^{\prime}:\tau\otimes\sigma
, then we may use the
outer inductive hypothesis
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}_{2},\;x:\tau,\;y:\sigma\models t[\gamma,\delta_{2}]:\kappa

and apply Lemma
85
.

–

If we have
u^{\prime}={\mathsf{let}}\;{x^{\prime}\otimes y^{\prime}}={u^{\prime\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u^{\prime\prime\prime}}
, applying the induction hypothesis, we have some
v
such
that

{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={u^{\prime\prime\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{t[\gamma,\delta_{2}]}\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}v\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime},x^{\prime}:\tau^{\prime},y^{\prime}:\sigma^{\prime}\models v:\kappa

We may thus conclude using the sequence of reductions

\begin{array}[]{rll}{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={{\mathsf{let}}\;{x^{\prime}\otimes y^{\prime}}={u^{\prime\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u^{\prime\prime\prime}}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{t[\gamma,\delta_{2}]}&\to_{\varepsilon}&{\mathsf{let}}\;{x^{\prime}\otimes y^{\prime}}={u^{\prime\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={u^{\prime\prime\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{t[\gamma,\delta_{2}]}}\\
&\to_{\beta\varepsilon}^{*}&{\mathsf{let}}\;{x^{\prime}\otimes y^{\prime}}={u^{\prime\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{v}\end{array}

and Lemma
85

–

We proceed similarly if
u^{\prime}={\mathsf{case}}(u^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime}.u^{\prime\prime\prime},y^{\prime}.u^{\prime\prime\prime\prime})
,
{\mathsf{let}}\;{()}={u^{\prime\prime}}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u^{\prime\prime\prime}}
or
\pi_{i}(u^{\prime\prime})
.

–

Finally, if
u^{\prime}=u^{\prime\prime}\otimes u^{\prime\prime\prime}
, we apply the outer induction hypothesis with the substitution
\gamma,\delta_{2},u^{\prime\prime}/x,u^{\prime\prime\prime}/y
to conclude.

Proof B.6

(Proof of Theorem
76
)
.

Instantiate Theorem
86
in the case of a trivial substitution (
t_{i}=x_{i}
and
u_{j}=a_{j}
)
using Corollary
84
and conclude with Theorem
83
.

Appendix C
Proof
Lemma
25
(on

\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-terms defining tree functions)

{defi}

Write
\sqsubseteq_{+}
for the least preorder relation over
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
types satisfying the following
for every types
\tau
and
\sigma

\tau,\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\otimes\sigma\quad\tau,\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\oplus\sigma\quad\tau,\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\with\sigma\quad\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\multimap\sigma\quad\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\to\sigma

We say that
\tau
is a
strictly positive subtype
of
\sigma
whenever
\tau\sqsubseteq_{+}\sigma
.

{defi}

A context
\Psi;\;\Delta
is called
consistent
if there is no term
t
such that
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash t:0
.

Lemma 87
.

A context
\Psi;\;\Delta
is inconsistent if and only if there is a neutral term
t
such that
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:0
.

Furthermore, if
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau
, the last typing rule applied has one premise
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\tau^{\prime}

and
\Psi;\;\Delta
is consistent, then so is
\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof C.1
.

The first point is an easy corollary of Theorem
76
.
The second point follows from a case analysis, using the following facts:

•

If
\Psi,\;\Psi^{\prime};\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}
is consistent, then so is
\Psi;\;\Delta
.

•

If
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}
is consistent and
\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash t:\tau
, then
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;x:\tau
is consistent.

Lemma 88
.

If
\Psi;\;\Delta
is consistent and
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau
, then
there is a variable in
\Psi;\;\Delta
of type
\sigma
with
\tau\sqsubseteq_{+}\sigma
.

Proof C.2
.

By induction on the judgement
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau
.

•

If the last rule applied was a variable lookup.

\frac{}{\Psi;\;x:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}x:\tau}\frac{}{\Psi,x:\tau;\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}x:\tau}

then the conclusion immediately follows.

•

The more interesting cases are those of the elimination rules for
\multimap
,
\otimes
and
\with
.

\small\frac{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\multimap\sigma\hskip 18.49988pt\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u:\tau}{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t\;u:\sigma}\frac{\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\otimes\sigma\hskip 18.49988pt\Psi;\;\Delta,\;x:\tau,\;y:\sigma\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\kappa}{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\kappa}\frac{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\with\sigma}{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}\pi_{1}(t):\tau}\frac{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\with\sigma}{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}\pi_{2}(t):\sigma}\frac{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma\oplus\tau\hskip 18.49988pt\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime},\;x:\sigma\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\kappa\hskip 18.49988pt\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime},\;y:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}v:\kappa}{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.u,y.v):\kappa}\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\mathbf{I}\hskip 18.49988pt\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{X}u:\kappa}{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{X}{\mathsf{let}}\;{()}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\kappa}\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:0}{\Psi\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{abort}}(t):\tau}

The treatment of
\with
and
\mathbf{I}
is rather straightforward and
0
is ruled out because
\Psi;\;\Delta
is assumed to be consistent, so we only explain the inductive step for

\multimap
,
\otimes
and
\oplus
.

–

If the last rule applied is the elimination of a linear arrow

\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\multimap\sigma\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u:\tau}{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t\;u:\sigma}

then the induction hypothesis applied to the first premise means that there is a variable
x
in
\Psi;\;\Delta
of type
\kappa

such that
\tau\multimap\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\kappa
, and we may conclude since
\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\multimap\sigma
.

–

If the last rule applied is the elimination of a tensor product

\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\tau\otimes\sigma\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta,\;x:\tau,\;y:\sigma\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\kappa}{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\kappa}

then the induction hypothesis applied to the first premise yields a variable
z
in
\Psi;\;\Delta,\;x:\tau,\;y:\sigma
of type
\zeta
with
\kappa\sqsubseteq_{+}\zeta
.
If
z\notin\{x,y\}
, then
z
occurs in
\Psi;\;\Delta
and we may conclude. Otherwise, suppose that
z=x
; applying the induction hypothesis to the second premise, we
know that
\tau\otimes\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\sqsubseteq_{+}\zeta^{\prime}
for a
\zeta^{\prime}
being the type of some variable in
\Delta^{\prime}
or a
\zeta^{\prime}=\oc\zeta^{\prime\prime}
with
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
being the type of some variable in
\Psi
.
Therefore, we may conclude since
{\kappa}\sqsubseteq_{+}{\tau}\sqsubseteq_{+}{\tau\otimes\sigma}\sqsubseteq_{+}{\zeta^{\prime}}
.
The case of
z=y
is treated similarly.

•

If the last rule applied is the elimination of a coproduct

\dfrac{\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma\oplus\tau\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime},\;x:\sigma\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\kappa\qquad\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime},\;y:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}v:\kappa}{\Psi;\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.u,y.v):\kappa}

then, by the inductive hypothesis applied to the second premise, there is
\zeta
with

\kappa\sqsubseteq_{+}\zeta
such that

(1)

either there is a variable in
\Psi;\;\Delta^{\prime}
with type
\zeta

(2)

or
\zeta=\sigma
.

In the first case, we may directly conclude.
Otherwise, the induction hypothesis applied to the first premise states that there is
\zeta^{\prime\prime}

with
\sigma\oplus\tau\sqsubseteq_{+}\zeta^{\prime\prime}
so that
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
is a type of some variable in
\Psi

or
\Delta
. Hence, we have
\kappa\sqsubseteq_{+}\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\sigma\oplus\tau\sqsubseteq_{+}\zeta^{\prime\prime}

and we may conclude.

Proposition 89
.

Fix a ranked alphabet
{\bf\Sigma}
.
The map
t\mapsto\underline{t}
taking trees
t\in\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
to their Church encodings
is a bijection.

Proof C.3

(Proof of Proposition
89
)
.

For the sake of this proof, let us assume that ranked alphabets
\bf\Sigma

are ordered. Recall that if
t\in\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
, we write

\underline{t}:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}
for its Church encoding.
When
\Sigma=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\}
,
\underline{t}
has
shape
\lambda^{\oc}a_{1}.\ldots.\lambda^{\oc}a_{k}.t^{\circ}
. for some neutral
term
t^{\circ}
. Let us adopt this notation for a map
t\mapsto t^{\circ}
,
mapping trees
t
to terms

\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash t^{\circ}:\mathbbl{o}
, and
let us abbreviate the sequence of
\lambda
-abstractions
\lambda^{\oc}a_{1}.\ldots.\lambda^{\oc}a_{k}.

as
\lambda^{\oc}{\bf\Sigma}
for arbitrary (ordered) ranked alphabets
\bf\Sigma
.

We use those conventions to show that the map
t\mapsto\underline{t}
is surjective
(where it is understood that the codomain consists of terms
up to
\beta\eta
-equivalence
.

Lemma 90
.

Fix a ranked alphabet
\widetilde{\bf\Sigma}
. For every typed normal term
u
, we have

(1)

if
\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u:\mathbbl{o}
, then there is
t\in\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})

such that
u=t^{\circ}
.

(2)

if
\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}
where the type
of
u
has
k
arguments, then there exists
a\in\Sigma
,
a list of trees
t_{1},\ldots,t_{{|\mathrm{ar}(a)|}-k}\in\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
such that

u=a\;t_{1}^{\circ}\;\ldots\;t_{{|\mathrm{ar}(a)|}-k}^{\circ}
.

Proof C.4
.

We proceed by induction over the typing judgment of the normal form
u
.
Many cases are easily seen to not arise (typically, constructor for various datatypes).
Most eliminators can also be ignored because of Lemma
88
. For instance, suppose

u={\mathsf{case}}(v,x.w,y.w^{\prime})
. Then it would means that we had some
\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}v:\tau\oplus\sigma

for some
\tau,\sigma
, but that cannot be the case as
\tau\oplus\sigma
is never a strictly positive subtype of some

\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}
.

As a consequence, there are only two cases of interest: the variable case and (linear) function application.

•

If
u
is a variable of type
\mathbbl{o}
, then the first result (and, as a consequence, the second) result is immediate:
u

is the Church encoding of a tree with a single leaf. If it is a variable of type
\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}
, the first claim
is vacuously true and the second is also immediate.

•

If
u
is a function application
v\;w
, then we have that
\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}v:\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}

in both cases. So we may apply the inductive hypothesis to obtain some
a\in\Sigma
and trees
t_{1},\ldots,t_{l}
such that

v=a\;t_{1}^{\circ}\;\ldots\;t_{l}^{\circ}
.
We also have that
\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}:w:\mathbbl{o}
, so we have some tree
t_{l+1}
such that
w=_{\beta\eta}t_{l+1}^{\circ}
.
Altogether, we thus have
u=a\;t_{1}^{\circ}\;\ldots\;t_{l+1}^{\circ}
as expected of the second item.
If the first item is not vacuously true, we have that
l+1={|A|}
, and thus,
a\;t_{1}^{\circ}\;\ldots\;t_{l+1}^{\circ}=(a(t_{1},\ldots,t_{l+1}))^{\circ}

as required.

Given two
ordered
ranked alphabets
{\bf\Sigma}
and
{\bf\Gamma}
, write
{\bf\Sigma}\otimes{\bf\Gamma}
for the ordered ranked alphabet
with letters
\Sigma+\Gamma
determined by
\mathsf{in}_{1}(a)<\mathsf{in}_{2}(b)
for
a\in\Sigma
,
b\in\Gamma
and where the order is lifted from
{\bf\Sigma}
and

{\bf\Gamma}
otherwise.

Lemma 91
.

Fix ranked alphabets
{\bf\Sigma},{\bf\Gamma}
.
If we have
\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash u:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Gamma}
, then there exists some
t\in\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma}\otimes{\bf\Gamma})
such that

u=_{\beta\eta}\lambda^{\oc}{\bf\Gamma}.t^{\circ}
.

Proof C.5
.

First, we use Theorem
76
to suppose that
u
is under normal form, and we proceed by induction over the size of
{\bf\Gamma}
.
If it is empty, then the result follows from the first item of Lemma
90
.
Otherwise
{\bf\Gamma}={\bf S}\otimes{\bf\Gamma^{\prime}}
for some singleton alphabet
{\bf S}
with letter
b
.
Then, a quick case analysis shows that, as in Lemma
90
, most cases can be ignored due to the typing of
u
, and because
of considerations based on Lemma
88
.
There is only one interesting case which is the non-linear
\lambda
-abstraction.

\dfrac{\widetilde{\bf\Sigma},\;\widetilde{\bf S};\;\cdot\vdash v:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Gamma}}{\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\cdot\vdash\lambda^{\oc}b.v:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf S\otimes\bf\Gamma}}

We may apply the induction hypothesis as
\widetilde{{\bf\Sigma}\otimes{\bf S}}=\widetilde{\bf\Sigma},\widetilde{\bf S}
and get that
u=_{\beta\eta}\lambda^{\oc}b.v=\lambda^{\oc}b.\lambda^{\oc}{\bf\Gamma}.t^{\circ}=\lambda^{\oc}{\bf S}\otimes{\bf\Gamma}.t^{\circ}
.

By instantiating this latest lemma with
{\bf\Gamma}
empty, we can thus deduce that the map
t\mapsto\underline{t}
is surjective.
We may also show that it is injective by exhibiting a left-inverse map, using a semantic interpretation of
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
into
{\sf Set}
:
with
{\bf\Sigma}
fixed, use the cartesian-closed structure and coproducts to interpret
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
with the interpretation of
\mathbbl{o}
being
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
.
This yields a map from terms
t:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}
to set-theoretic functions
(\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\to\ldots\to\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma}))\to\ldots\to\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
,
where the arguments correspond to the arity of tree constructors; feed the actual constructors to this function to recover a tree in
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
.

It is straightforward to check that this map is indeed a left inverse of
t\mapsto\underline{t}
, by induction over
t
.
Hence the map
t\mapsto\underline{t}
is bijective.

Lemma 92
.

Let
\tau=\kappa_{1}\to\ldots\to\kappa_{k}\to\kappa^{\prime}
be a type
and
s
a distinguished variable of type
\tau
.
Let
\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}
be a ranked alphabet such that
{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau
is consistent.
Then, if there is
k^{\prime}<k
such that
{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\kappa_{k^{\prime}+1}\to\ldots\to\kappa_{k}\to\kappa
, there are also
terms
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k^{\prime}}
such that

t=_{\beta\eta}s\;d_{1}\;\ldots\;d_{k^{\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}d_{i}:\kappa_{i}\qquad\text{for $i\in\{1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}$}

Proof C.6
.

By induction over
k^{\prime}
. Note that
t
being neutral is essential here.

Lemma 93
.

Let
\tau=\kappa_{1}\to\ldots\to\kappa_{k}\to\kappa^{\prime}
be a type
with
\kappa^{\prime}
purely linear
and
s
a distinguished variable of type
\tau
.
Let
\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}
be a ranked alphabet such that
{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau
is consistent and

t
be a term such that
{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma
for some

\sigma
such that
\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\kappa^{\prime}
or of the shape
\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}
.
Then, there are terms
o
,
d_{1}
, …,
d_{k}
such that
t=_{\beta\eta}o\;(s\;d_{1}\;\ldots\;d_{k})
and

{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash o:\kappa^{\prime}\multimap\sigma\qquad\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}d_{i}:\kappa_{i}\quad\text{for $i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}$}

Proof C.7
.

We proceed by induction over a derivation of
{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma
.
Note that to apply the induction hypothesis, we need to ensure that every context under consideration is consistent.
We keep this check implicit as it always follows from Lemma
87
.

•

If the last rule applied is a variable lookup, then the term in question must be
s
itself.
Furthermore, we must have
k=0
, so we may simply take
o=\lambda x.x
to conclude.

•

If the last rule considered is the following instance of the application rule

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma^{\prime}\multimap\sigma\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u:\sigma^{\prime}}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t\;u:\sigma}

then, by Lemma
88
(applied on the first premise), it means that we have

\text{either}\penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma^{\prime}\multimap\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\qquad\text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma\multimap\sigma^{\prime}=\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}

In the first case, we can further see that
\sigma^{\prime}\multimap\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\kappa^{\prime}
, so in both cases the induction hypothesis can be applied to the first premise to yield
some
o^{\prime}
and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
such that
o^{\prime}\;(s\;d_{1}\ldots d_{k})=_{\beta\eta}t
, and we may set
o=\lambda s.\;o^{\prime}\;s\;u
to conclude.

•

If the last rule considered is the following instance of the application rule

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma^{\prime}\to\sigma\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u:\sigma^{\prime}}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t\;u:\sigma}

then, by Lemma
88
(applied on the first premise), it means that we have

\text{either}\penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma^{\prime}\to\sigma\sqsubseteq_{+}\tau\qquad\text{or}\penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma\to\sigma^{\prime}=\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}

The second alternative is absurd, and the first leads to
\sigma=\kappa^{\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\kappa_{k}
.
Therefore, we may apply Lemma
92
to get terms
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k-1}
in normal form such that
t=_{\beta\eta}s\;d_{1}\;\ldots\;d_{k-1}
. We then set
d_{k}
to be
u
and
o
to be the identity to conclude.

•

If the last rule considered is the other instance of the application rule

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\Delta\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma^{\prime}\multimap\sigma\qquad\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\Delta^{\prime},\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}u:\sigma^{\prime}}{\widetilde{\bf\Sigma};\;\Delta,\;\Delta^{\prime},\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t\;u:\sigma}

by Lemma
88
applied to the first premise, we know that
\sigma^{\prime}=\mathbbl{o}
.
Therefore, we may apply the induction hypothesis to the second premise to obtain

d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
and
o^{\prime}
such that
u=_{\beta\eta}o^{\prime}\;(s\;d_{1}\;\ldots\;d_{k})
, in which case,
t\;u=_{\beta\eta}(\lambda x.\;t\;(o\;x))\;(s\;d_{1}\;\ldots\;d_{k})
. We conclude by setting
o=\lambda z.\;t\;(o^{\prime}\;z)
.

•

If the last rule considered is the following instance of the elimination of tensor products

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\zeta_{1}\otimes\zeta_{2}\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;x_{1}:\zeta_{1},\;x_{2}:\zeta_{2}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\sigma}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\sigma}

then, by the induction hypothesis (which is applicable because of Lemma
88
),
there are
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
and
o^{\prime}
such that
t=_{\beta\eta}o^{\prime}\;(s\;d_{1}\;\ldots\;d_{k})
, in which case,
we conclude by setting
o=\lambda z.\;{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={o^{\prime}\;z}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}
.

•

The last rule considered cannot be the following instance of the elimination of tensor products

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\zeta_{1}\otimes\zeta_{2}\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau,\;x_{1}:\zeta_{1},\;x_{2}:\zeta_{2}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\sigma}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{let}}\;{x\otimes y}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\sigma}

as Lemma
88
would require that
\zeta_{1}\otimes\zeta_{2}
be a strictly positive subtype of some
\mathbbl{o}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbbl{o}
.

•

If the last rule considered types a projection

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\sigma_{1}\with\sigma_{2}}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}\pi_{i}(t):\sigma_{i}}

then the induction hypothesis yields
terms
o^{\prime}
and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
such that
t=_{\beta\eta}o^{\prime}\;(s\;d_{1}\ldots d_{k})
. We may set
o=\lambda z.\;\pi_{i}(o^{\prime}\;z)
to conclude.

•

If the last rule applied is an elimination of a coproduct

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\zeta_{1}\oplus\zeta_{2}\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;x:\zeta_{1}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\sigma\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;y:\zeta_{2}\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}v:\sigma}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.u,y.v):\sigma}

then, the induction hypothesis (applicable because of Lemma
88
) yields
terms
o^{\prime}
and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
such that
t=_{\beta\eta}o^{\prime}\;(s\;d_{1}\ldots d_{k})
. We may set
o=\lambda z.\;{\mathsf{case}}(o^{\prime}\;z,x.u,y.v)
to conclude.

•

The last rule applied cannot be one of the following instances of the elimination of a coproduct because of Lemma
88
applied to the first premise:

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\zeta_{1}\oplus\zeta_{2}\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau,\;x:\zeta_{1}\vdash_{X}u:\sigma\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;y:\zeta_{2}\vdash_{X}v:\sigma}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{X}{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.u,y.v):\sigma}

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\zeta_{1}\oplus\zeta_{2}\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\;x:\zeta_{1}\vdash_{X}u:\sigma\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau,\;y:\zeta_{2}\vdash_{X}v:\sigma}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{X}{\mathsf{case}}(t,x.u,y.v):\sigma}

•

If the last rule applied is an elimination of
\mathbf{I}

\dfrac{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;s:\tau\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}t:\mathbf{I}\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\;\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}u:\sigma}{{\bf\widetilde{\Sigma}};\;\cdot\;\vdash_{\mathrm{NE}}{\mathsf{let}}\;{()}={t}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}:\sigma}

then, the induction hypothesis (applicable because of Lemma
88
) yields
terms
o^{\prime}
and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k}
such that
t=_{\beta\eta}o^{\prime}\;(s\;d_{1}\ldots d_{k})
. We may set
o=\lambda z.\;{\mathsf{let}}\;{()}={o^{\prime}\;z}\;{\mathsf{in}}\;{u}
to conclude.

•

The last rule applied cannot be one of the other instances of
\mathbf{I}
because of Lemma
88
.

•

Finally, since the context under consideration are assumed to be consistent, the last rule applied cannot be an elimination of
0
.

Lemma 94
.

Let
{\bf\Sigma}
and
{\bf\Gamma}
be ranked alphabets.
If the context
{\bf\widetilde{\Gamma}};\;s:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]
is inconsistent, then

\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})=\varnothing
.

Proof C.8
.

Let us write
{\llbracket-\rrbracket}
for a semantic interpretation of
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
types as (classical) propositions
following the usual type/proposition mapping (i.e.,
{\llbracket\tau\multimap\sigma\rrbracket}={\llbracket\tau\to\sigma\rrbracket}={\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}\Rightarrow{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket}
,

{\llbracket\tau\otimes\sigma\rrbracket}={\llbracket\tau\with\sigma\rrbracket}={\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}\wedge{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket}
,
{\llbracket 0\rrbracket}=\bot
, …) and such that

{\llbracket\mathbbl{o}\rrbracket}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Gamma})\neq\varnothing

It is easy to check that, under the usual conjunctive interpretation of contexts, if
\Psi;\;\Delta\vdash t:\tau
, then

{\llbracket\Psi\rrbracket}\wedge{\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket}\Rightarrow{\llbracket\tau\rrbracket}
holds.
Further, our choice for
{\llbracket\mathbbl{o}\rrbracket}
means that
{\llbracket\bf\widetilde{\Gamma}\rrbracket}
holds, so, if our
context
{\bf\widetilde{\Gamma}};\;s:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]
is inconsistent,
\neg{\llbracket\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]\rrbracket}
holds.
Now, assume that
\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})
has an inhabitant
t
. There is a corresponding Church encoding
\underline{t}

of type
\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}
, which has also type
\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]
. Hence,
{\llbracket\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]\rrbracket}
also holds,
leading to a contradiction.

Proof C.9

(Proof of Lemma
25
)
.

Assume that we have a closed
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
-term
t
of type
\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]\multimap\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Gamma}
with

\mathbf{Tree}({\bf\Sigma})\neq\varnothing
, so that we may safely assume
{\bf\widetilde{\Gamma}};\;s:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]
to be consistent.
By
\eta
-expansion, we have is of the shape

t=_{\beta\eta}\lambda w.\lambda^{\oc}b_{1}.\ldots\lambda^{\oc}b_{k}.t\;w\;b_{1}\ldots b_{k}

and
{\bf\widetilde{\Gamma}};\;s:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]\vdash t\;w\;b_{1}\ldots b_{k}:\mathbbl{o}
.
By normalization of
\lambda\ell^{\oplus\with}
, there is
t^{\prime}
such that

t^{\prime}=_{\beta\eta}t\;w\;b_{1}\ldots b_{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\bf\widetilde{\Gamma}};\;s:\mathrm{Tree}_{\bf\Sigma}[\kappa]\vdash_{\mathrm{NF}}t^{\prime}:\mathbbl{o}

For the latter, note that an easy case analysis shows that every (open) term of type
\mathbbl{o}
is in fact neutral, so we may conclude by applying
Lemma
93
and the fact that
=_{\beta\eta}
is a congruence over terms.

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
47
(monoidal closure)

Here we give a detailed proof of Theorem
47
by establishing
a tensor/hom adjunction

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(C\otimes A,B\right)\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(C,A\multimap B\right)

natural in
C
.
While we will not devote too much space to
checking naturality down to low-level details, we have attempted to break down
this verification into manageable chunks. In particular, our notations are going
to stress the functoriality of the various intermediate expressions in our
computation. Some lemmas will be useful for this.

Lemma 95
.

We can lift every functor
F:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\sf Set}
to a functor

\langle\with\rangle F:(\mathcal{C}_{\with})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\sf Set}
such that for

(C_{i})\in\mathsf{Obj}(C)^{I}
,

\langle\with\rangle F\left(\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}\right)\quad=\quad\sum_{i\in I}F(C_{i})

and in such a way that if
F,G:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\sf Set}
are naturally
isomorphic, so are
\langle\with\rangle F
and
\langle\with\rangle G
.

Proof D.1
.

Since
(\mathcal{C}_{\with})^{\mathrm{op}}=(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}})_{\oplus}
by duality, the existence
of
\langle\with\rangle F
is simply the universal property of the

coproduct
completion! Concretely, consider a morphism

f=\displaystyle\left(\varphi_{f}:I\to J,\;\left(f_{i}:C^{\prime}_{\varphi_{f}(i)}\to C_{i}\right)_{{i\in I}}\right)\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\with}}\left(\bigwith_{j\in J}C^{\prime}_{j}\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}\right)

Its functorial image is given by

\langle\with\rangle F(f)\quad:\quad(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}F(C_{i})\quad\mapsto\quad(\varphi_{f}(i),F(f_{i})(x))\in\sum_{j\in J}F(C^{\prime}_{j})

Using this explicit expression, one can check that if

\eta_{X}:F(X)\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}G(X)
is a natural isomorphism (for
X

varying over
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
), then

(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}F(C_{i})\quad\mapsto\quad(i,\eta_{C_{i}}(x))\in\sum_{i\in I}G(C_{i})

defines a natural isomorphism between
\langle\with\rangle F
and

\langle\with\rangle G
.

Lemma 96
.

We can lift every functor
F:(\mathcal{C}_{\with})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\sf Set}
to

\langle\oplus\rangle F:(\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\sf Set}
in such a way
that if
F,G:(\mathcal{C}_{\with})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\sf Set}
are naturally isomorphic, so are

\langle\oplus\rangle F
and
\langle\oplus\rangle G
, and with the action on
objects

\langle\oplus\rangle F\left(\bigoplus_{u\in U}\bigwith_{x\in X_{u}}C_{x}\right)\quad=\quad\prod_{u\in U}F\left(\bigwith_{x\in X_{u}}C_{x}\right)

Proof D.2
.

Write
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}\cong\mathcal{D}_{\oplus}
with
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}_{\with}
. The
statement is a special case of a property that works for any category
\mathcal{D}
;
analogously (and dually) to the previous lemma, it corresponds to the
universal property of the product completion.

Before we can state the next lemma, we first need a few definitions. Let

\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}:\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}\to\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}\quad\text{such that}\quad\iota_{\oplus\with}=\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}\circ\iota_{\oplus}

be the full and faithful embedding be obtained by applying the universal
property of
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}
to the coproduct-preserving functor

\iota_{\oplus\with}
. Furthermore, the isomorphism

\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}\cong(\mathcal{C}_{\with})_{\oplus}
entails the existence of
another full and faithful functor

\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}:\mathcal{C}_{\with}\to\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}\quad\text{such
that}\quad\iota_{\oplus\with}=\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}\circ\iota_{\with}

corresponding to the canonical embedding
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}_{\oplus}
for

\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}_{\with}
. Both those functors have straightforward explicit
constructions, whose actions on objects are

\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}\left(\bigoplus_{i\in I}C_{i}\right)\quad=\quad\bigoplus_{i\in I}\bigwith_{j\in\{*\}}C_{i}\qquad\qquad\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}\left(\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}\right)\quad=\quad\bigoplus_{j\in\{*\}}\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}

(where the (co)product notation is syntactic sugar for indexed families).

Lemma 97
.

For any
D\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{\oplus})
, there is a natural isomorphism (with domain

\mathcal{C}_{\with}
)

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(-),\iota^{\oplus}_{\with}(D)\right)\quad\cong\quad\langle\with\rangle\left[\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(-),D\right)\right]

Proof D.3
.

Let
\displaystyle C=\bigwith_{i\in I}C_{i}
and

\displaystyle D=\bigoplus_{k\in K}D_{k}
for
C_{i},D_{k}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.
By definition,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(C),\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(D)\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{p\in\{*\}}\sum_{k\in K}\prod_{q\in\{*\}}\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{i},D_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{k\in K}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{i},D_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(C_{i}),D\right)

This establishes the isomorphism for each object of
\mathcal{C}_{\with}
. The
naturality condition can be recast as the commutation of the following
diagram:

\textstyle{\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(C),\iota^{\oplus}_{\with}(D)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\circ f}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\displaystyle\sum_{i,k}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{i},D_{k}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\sim}
\scriptstyle{\sim\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(C_{i}),D\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i,g)\mapsto(\varphi_{f}(i),g\circ f_{i})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(C^{\prime}),\iota^{\oplus}_{\with}(D)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\sum_{j,k}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C^{\prime}_{j},D_{k}\right)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(C^{\prime}_{j}),D\right)}
\scriptstyle{\sim\hskip 16.38895pt}

Fortunately, it can be checked from the definition of composition in

\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
that both paths in this diagrams denote
the same map, namely
(i,k,h)\mapsto(\varphi_{f}(i),k,h\circ f_{i})
.

Proof D.4

(Proof of Theorem
47
)
.

Let
A,B,C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with})
. By definition, we can write

A=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{u\in U}\bigwith_{x\in X_{u}}A_{x}\qquad B=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}B_{y}\qquad C=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{w\in W}\bigwith_{z\in Z_{w}}C_{z}

where
A_{x},B_{y},C_{z}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Using the definition of
\otimes
in
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
and of
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(-,-\right)
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(C\otimes A,B\right)

\displaystyle={\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\bigoplus_{(w,u)\in W\times U}\bigwith_{(z,x)\in Z_{w}\times X_{u}}C_{z}\otimes A_{x}\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}B_{y}\right)}

\displaystyle={\displaystyle\prod_{w,u}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\sum_{z,x}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{z}\otimes A_{x},\;B_{y}\right)}

\displaystyle\cong{\displaystyle\prod_{w\in W}\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\sum_{x\in X_{u}}\sum_{z\in Z_{w}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(C_{z}\otimes A_{x},\;B_{y}\right)}

Using the operators from Lemmas
95

and
96
, the last expression above can be turned into a
functor in
C
, and the isomorphism is then natural in
C
, that is:

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(-\otimes A,B\right)\quad\cong\quad\langle\oplus\rangle\left[\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\sum_{x\in X_{u}}\langle\with\rangle\left[\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(-\otimes A_{x},\;B_{y}\right)\right]\right]

(Any reader who is not convinced that rearranging the indexing of
sums/products is innocuous with respect to the morphisms in

\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}
can check the naturality by a brute-force computation.)

For any
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, we have a sequence of natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(-\otimes A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(-\otimes A^{\prime}),\iota_{\oplus}(B^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(-)\otimes\iota_{\oplus}(A^{\prime}),\iota_{\oplus}(B^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(-),\iota_{\oplus}(A^{\prime})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(B^{\prime})\right)

by using the strong monoidality and full faithfulness of
\iota_{\oplus}
as
well as the assumption on internal homsets in
\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}
.
Lemma
95
allows us to lift natural isomorphisms through

\langle\with\rangle
; combined with Lemma
97
, this gives
us

\displaystyle\langle\with\rangle\left[\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(-\otimes A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\cong\langle\with\rangle\left[\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus}}\left(\iota_{\oplus}(-),\iota_{\oplus}(A^{\prime})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(B^{\prime})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(-),\iota^{\oplus}_{\with}(\iota_{\oplus}(A^{\prime})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(B^{\prime}))\right)

Let us introduce the abbreviation

A^{\prime}\rightrightarrows B^{\prime}=\iota_{\with}^{\oplus}(\iota_{\oplus}(A^{\prime})\multimap\iota_{\oplus}(B^{\prime}))
.
Using Proposition
28
and the dual of
Remark
29
for products, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(-\otimes A,B\right)

\displaystyle\cong\langle\oplus\rangle\left[\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\sum_{x\in X_{u}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(-)\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;A_{x}\rightrightarrows B_{y}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\cong\langle\oplus\rangle\left[\prod_{u\in U}\sum_{v\in V}\prod_{y\in Y_{v}}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(-)\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\bigoplus_{x\in X_{u}}A_{x}\rightrightarrows B_{y}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\cong\;\cdots

\displaystyle\cong\langle\oplus\rangle\left[\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(\iota^{\prime}_{\oplus}(-)\text{{\Huge{,}}}\;\overbrace{\bigwith_{u\in U}\bigoplus_{v\in V}\bigwith_{y\in Y_{v}}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{u}}A_{x}\rightrightarrows B_{y}}^{A\multimap B}\right)\right]

To conclude, it suffices to show that the last functor in the above computation
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{\oplus\with}}\left(-,A\multimap B\right)
. The
isomorphism can seen as an instance of the universal property of the coproduct,
cf. Remark
29
which unfortunately only states naturality in
the
“wrong”
argument for our purposes. The remaining naturality
condition can be verified routinely; we leave it to the reader.

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
20

(building functors from
\mathcal{SR}
)

Let us fix a an symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbf{I})
with an
internal monoid
(M,\mu,\eta)
.
We call the functors built from the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{C}

“tensorial functors”
since the monoidal product
\otimes
is sometimes
called a tensor product. They play a central role in our proof and are more
precisely defined as follows.

{defi}

The
tensorial expressions
over a finite indexing set
I
are freely
inductively generated as follows:

•

\mathbf{I}
is a tensorial expression over
\varnothing
;

•

i
is a tensorial expression over
\{i\}
;

•

if
e,e^{\prime}
are tensorial expressions over
I
and
I^{\prime}
respectively, with

I\cap I^{\prime}=\varnothing
, then
(e\otimes e^{\prime})
is a tensorial expression
over
I\cup I^{\prime}
.

In other words, a tensorial expression over
I
is a binary tree whose leaves
are labeled either by
\mathbf{I}
or by
i\in I
, such that each element
i

appears exactly once.

A tensorial expression
e
over
I
induces a functor
F_{e}:\mathcal{C}^{I}\to\mathcal{C}
in
an obvious way. The functors thus obtained are called
tensorial
functors
. A tensorial functor
F_{e}:\mathcal{C}^{I}\to\mathcal{C}
is
ordered
when

I
is endowed with the total order that corresponds to the infix order on the

I
-labeled leaves of the expression
e
.

Of course, the basic intuition is that over a given totally ordered indexing
set, two ordered tensorial functors express
“the same thing”
up to
inessential bracketing, while two unordered tensorial functors should be morally
the same
“up to permutation”
. This is expressed formally as a natural
isomorphism between functors, but
Mac Lane’s coherence theorem
gives us
something stronger: the natural isomorphisms can be chosen
canonically
.

Theorem 98

(Coherence for symmetric monoidal categories
[
ML98
, §XI.1
(Theorem 1)]
)
.

There exists a map that sends each triple
(I,e,e^{\prime})
, where
I
is a finite
set and
e,e^{\prime}
are tensorial expressions over
I
, to a
natural isomorphism from the tensorial functor
F_{e}
to
F_{e^{\prime}}
, which we
call a
canonical isomorphism
, such that:

•

identities, associators, unitors and symmetries are canonical
isomorphisms, i.e. are in the image of this map;

•

composing the image of
(I,e,e^{\prime})
by this map with the image of

(I,e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})
yields the image of
(I,e,e^{\prime\prime})
– in other words, canonical
isomorphisms are closed under composition;

•

canonical isomorphisms are also closed under monoidal product.

If
F,G:\mathcal{C}^{I}\to\mathcal{C}
are
ordered
tensorial functors for the same
total order on
I
, the construction of the canonical isomorphism between
F

and
G
works in any monoidal category, not necessarily symmetric. Indeed,
general monoidal categories enjoy a coherence theorem of their
own
[
ML98
, §VII.2]
, which involves its own canonical isomorphisms; thanks
to the uniqueness clauses in the coherence theorems with and without symmetry,
one can check that the two notions of canonical isomorphism coincide for ordered
tensorial functors. The ordered case is important for us because even though our
monoidal category is symmetric, the internal monoid
(M,\mu,\eta)
that we are
given need not be
commutative
. What the axioms for monoid objects

do
state, however, is a suitable form of associativity, a consequence of
which is that
n
-ary products are somehow
“independent of bracketing”
.
Formally speaking:

{defi}

To any tensorial expression
e
, we associate an
“
e
-fold monoid
multiplication”
morphism
\mu^{(e)}:F_{e}((M)_{i\in I})\to M
inductively:

\mu^{(\mathbf{I})}=\eta\qquad\mu^{(i)}=\mathrm{id}\ \text{for}\ I=\{i\}\qquad\mu^{(e\otimes e^{\prime})}=\mu\circ\left(\mu^{(e)}\otimes\mu^{(e^{\prime})}\right)

Theorem 99

(General associativity law
[
ML98
, §VII.3]
)
.

Let
I
be a totally ordered finite set,
F_{e},F_{e^{\prime}}:\mathcal{C}^{I}\to\mathcal{C}
be two

ordered
tensorial functors and
\Xi:F_{e}\Rightarrow F_{e^{\prime}}
be their
canonical natural isomorphism. Then
\mu^{(e)}=\mu^{(e^{\prime})}\circ\Xi_{\vec{M}}
where
\vec{M}=(M)_{i\in I}
.

We find it convenient to work directly with tensorial functors in the rest of
this appendix, leaving the tensorial expressions that define them implicit.
Therefore, we write
\mu^{(F)}
instead of
\mu^{(e)}
when
F=F_{e}
; strictly
speaking, two expressions could define the same functor for accidental reasons,
but for our purposes, the right choice of
e
can always be inferred from the
context. Similarly, we shall speak of
the
canonical isomorphism between
two tensorial functors
\mathcal{C}^{I}\to\mathcal{C}
.
In accordance with
Section

2.1
, we write

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}=(\dots(Y_{1}\otimes Y_{2})\otimes\dots)\otimes Y_{n}\qquad M^{\otimes n}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}M

and this will be used to define ordered tensorial functors. Furthermore, for
each finite set
I
, we fix an arbitrary choice of tensorial functor

\bigotimes_{i\in I}(-):\mathcal{C}^{I}\to\mathcal{C}
, and denote by
\bigotimes_{i\in I}Y_{i}
the image of
(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
by this functor.

After these general preliminaries, let us focus on the specific study of the
category
\mathcal{SR}(\Gamma)
for a fixed finite alphabet
\Gamma
.

{defi}

For
t\in[R\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R^{\prime}]
– recall that this implies
t:R^{\prime}\to(\Gamma+R)^{*}
, let
\partial(t)\subseteq R
be the set of register variables that do
not occur in any
t(r^{\prime})
for
r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
.

{defi}

Given
w\in(\Gamma+R)^{*}
, we write

\bigotimes_{r\looparrowleft w}Y_{r}\;=\;\bigotimes_{i=1}^{{|w|}}\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}&\text{when}\ w[i]\in\mathsf{in}_{1}(\Gamma)\\
Y_{r}&\text{when}\ w[i]=\mathsf{in}_{2}(r)\ \text{for}\ r\in R\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\qquad M^{\looparrowleft w}\;=\;\bigotimes_{r\looparrowleft w}M

Lemma 100
.

For
t\in[R\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R^{\prime}]
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\bigotimes_{r\in R}Y_{r}\quad\cong\quad\bigotimes_{r\in\partial(t)}Y_{r}\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}\bigotimes_{r\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}Y_{r}

Proof E.1
.

To apply Mac Lane’s coherence theorem, we just have to check that the
right-hand side defines a tensorial functor with indexing set
R
. This
amounts to the equality

R=\partial(t)\cup\bigcup_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}\{r\mid\exists i.\,t(r^{\prime})[i]=\mathsf{in}_{2}(r)\}

where, to ensure that no index in
R
is repeated, the union must be

disjoint
and the letters of
t(r^{\prime})
that are in
\mathsf{in}_{2}(R)
must all be
distinct. Those conditions are consequences of copylessness, while the
equality itself is essentially the definition of
\partial(t)
.

We are now in a position to build the functor promised in
Theorem
20
. Following the statement of this theorem, we
fix a family of morphisms
(m_{c})\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\mathbf{I},M\right)^{\Gamma}
, and assume
that
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,\mathbf{I})
is affine. Thus, we may write
\left\langle\right\rangle_{A}:A\to\mathbf{I}
for the terminal morphism from
A
, omiting the subscript
A
when it can
be inferred from the context.

{defi}

We define a map on objects
F:R\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{SR})\mapsto M^{\otimes R}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

As for morphisms, given a register transition
t\in[R\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R^{\prime}]
, we set

F(t)
to be

F(R)=M^{\otimes R}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}M^{\otimes\partial(t)}\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{{|t(r^{\prime})|}}X_{r^{\prime},i}\xrightarrow{\;\left\langle\right\rangle\otimes\widetilde{F}(t)\;}\mathbf{I}\otimes M^{\otimes R^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}M^{\otimes R^{\prime}}=F^{\prime}(R)

where the left arrow instantiates the canonical isomorphism of
Lemma
100
, with

X_{r^{\prime},i}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{I}&\text{when}\ t(r^{\prime})[i]\in\mathsf{in}_{1}(\Gamma)\\
M&\text{otherwise, i.e.}\ t(r^{\prime})[i]\in\mathsf{in}_{2}(R)\end{cases}\qquad\text{so that}\qquad\bigotimes_{i=1}^{{|t(r^{\prime})|}}X_{r^{\prime},i}=M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}

and in the middle arrow,
\left\langle\right\rangle:M^{\otimes\partial(t)}\to\mathbf{I}
is the
terminal morphism and

\widetilde{F}(t)=\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}\left(\widetilde{F}(t)_{r^{\prime}}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{{|t(r^{\prime})|}}X_{r^{\prime},i}\xrightarrow{\;\;\bigotimes_{i}f_{r^{\prime},i}\;\;}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{{|t(r^{\prime})|}}M=M^{\otimes{|t(r^{\prime})|}}\xrightarrow{\;\;\mu^{({|t(r^{\prime})|})}\;\;}M\right)

where for
i\in\{1,\ldots,{|t(r^{\prime})|}\}
, we pick

f_{r^{\prime},i}=\begin{cases}m_{c}&\text{when}\ t(r^{\prime})[i]=\mathsf{in}_{1}(c)\ \text{for}\ c\in\Gamma\\
\mathrm{id}_{M}&\text{otherwise, i.e.}\ t(r^{\prime})[i]\in\mathsf{in}_{2}(R)\end{cases}

(recall that
m_{c}:\mathbf{I}\to M
is the prescribed functorial image, by the

F
that we are defining, of the register transition
\widehat{c}\in[\varnothing\to_{\mathcal{SR}}\{\bullet\}]
).

The tedious part in proving Theorem
20
is checking that
the above definition works.

.

F
introduced in Definition
E
is a functor.

Figure 22.
The commutativity of the outer square of this diagram establishes
Proposition
101
. The text in the inner
squares explains why they commute; the proof text for
Proposition
101
defines
N
and gives further
justifications (in particular for the existence of the canonical
isomorphisms denoted by
\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}
).

Proof E.2
.

Let
t\in[R\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R^{\prime}]
and
t^{\prime}\in[R^{\prime}\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R^{\prime\prime}]
; we want to reason on

F(t^{\prime})\circ F(t)
to show that it is equal to
F(t^{\prime}\circ t)
. Beware: we
write
t^{\prime}\circ t
for composition of (copyless) register transitions in the
category
\mathcal{SR}
, and will employ the notation
t(r^{\prime})
for set-theoretic
application (
t:R^{\prime}\to(\Gamma+R)^{*}
), but we do
not
have
(t^{\prime}\circ t)(r^{\prime})=t^{\prime}(t(r^{\prime}))
– indeed, the two sides of the equality are not
even well-defined for
r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
! Since
t^{\prime}\circ t
is in
[R\to_{\mathcal{SR}}R^{\prime\prime}]
,
it is a set-theoretic map
R^{\prime\prime}\to(\Gamma+R)^{*}
.

To prove this, we first reduce our goal to
Lemma
102
, and then prove that lemma. The
reduction is given by the commutative diagram of

Figure

22
(with
N
to be defined later). Indeed, while
the morphism on the top is
F(t^{\prime})\circ F(t)
, the one defined by the three
other sides of the outermost square is

M^{\otimes R}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}M^{\otimes\partial(t^{\prime}\circ t)}\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}M^{\looparrowleft(t^{\prime}\circ t)(r^{\prime\prime})}\xrightarrow{\;\left\langle\right\rangle\otimes\widetilde{F}(t^{\prime}\circ t)\;}\mathbf{I}\otimes M^{\otimes R^{\prime\prime}}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}M^{\otimes R^{\prime\prime}}

thanks to the closure of canonical isomorphisms of tensorial functors under
composition. Since these canonical isomorphisms are also unique, we can equate
that with the expression for
F(t^{\prime}\circ t)
given in
Definition
E
, hence
F(t^{\prime})\circ F(t)=F(t^{\prime}\circ t)
.

The main justifications that are missing from
Figure

22

are the commutation of one square treated in
Lemma
102
, and the existence of the various
canonical isomorphisms involved. The ones in the right column are
just unitors, so let us focus on the left column.

•

The top left isomorphism is an instance of Lemma
100
.

•

The next one (skipping the equality) is provided by
Lemma
102
.

•

Then the penultimate one is just the inverse of an associator (see

Section

2.4
), of the form

\alpha^{-1}_{A,B,C}\;:\;A\otimes(B\otimes C)\;\to\;(A\otimes B)\otimes C

•

Finally, the last one reduces to
M^{\otimes\partial(t)}\otimes N\cong M^{\otimes\partial(t^{\prime}\circ t)}
.

To prove the latter, we must first clarify it by defining
N
consistently
with Lemma
102
:

N=\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}

We then see that the canonical isomorphism that we are looking for is the
instantiation to the constant family
(M)_{r\in\partial(t^{\prime}\circ t)}
of the
following natural isomorphism in
Y_{r}
for
r\in\partial(t^{\prime}\circ t)
:

\bigotimes_{r\in\partial(t)}Y_{r}\;\otimes\;\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})}\bigotimes_{r\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}Y_{r}\quad\cong\quad\bigotimes_{r\in\partial(t^{\prime}\circ t)}Y_{r}

This is a consequence of an elementary combinatorial fact:

\partial(t)\cup\bigcup_{r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})}\{r\mid\exists i\in\{1,\ldots,{|t(r^{\prime})|}\}.\;t(r^{\prime})[i]=\mathsf{in}_{2}(r)\}\quad=\quad\partial(t^{\prime}\circ t)

Informally speaking, this means that a register is thrown away by
t^{\prime}\circ t

if and only if is either thrown away by
t
, or used by
t
to compute a value
that is then discarded by
t^{\prime}
. Furthermore, thanks to copylessness, the union
is
disjoint
and for each
r
in the union over
r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})
,
there is a
single

(r^{\prime},i)
such that
t(r^{\prime})[i]=\mathsf{in}_{2}(r)
; those
properties are necessary to get the natural isomorphism. We leave a formal
proof of this combinatorial identity to the reader.

This being done, all that remains to conclude our proof is to show
Lemma
102
below.

Lemma 102
.

The diagram of
Figure

23
commutes, with the following
definitions:

N=\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}\qquad O_{r^{\prime\prime}}=\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\looparrowleft t^{\prime}(r^{\prime\prime})}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}\qquad\psi_{r^{\prime\prime}}=\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\looparrowleft t^{\prime}(r^{\prime\prime})}\widetilde{F}(t)_{r^{\prime}}

\textstyle{\displaystyle\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\widetilde{F}(t)=\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}\widetilde{F}(t)_{r^{\prime}}}
naturality / Lemma
100
\textstyle{M^{\otimes R^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{F(t^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\quad\text{definition of}\ F(t^{\prime})}
\textstyle{M^{\otimes R^{\prime\prime}}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle N\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}O_{r^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\ldots)\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}}\psi_{r^{\prime\prime}}}
\otimes
is a bifunctor and
\mathbf{I}
is a terminal object
\textstyle{\displaystyle M^{\otimes\partial(t^{\prime})}\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}M^{\looparrowleft t^{\prime}(r^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left\langle\right\rangle\otimes\widetilde{F}(t^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{I}\otimes M^{\otimes R^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\displaystyle N\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}O_{r^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\left\langle\right\rangle\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}(\widetilde{F}(t^{\prime})_{r^{\prime\prime}}\circ\psi_{r^{\prime\prime}})}
manipulations on monoid objects, explained in main text
\textstyle{\mathbf{I}\otimes M^{\otimes R^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle N\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}M^{\looparrowleft(t\circ t^{\prime})(r^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\left\langle\right\rangle\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}\widetilde{F}(t^{\prime}\circ t)_{r^{\prime\prime}}\;=\;\left\langle\right\rangle\otimes F(t^{\prime}\circ t)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{I}\otimes M^{\otimes R^{\prime\prime}}}

Figure 23.
Lemma
102
defines
N
,
O_{r^{\prime\prime}}
,
and
\psi_{r^{\prime\prime}}
, and proves that this diagram commutes.

Proof E.3
.

In addition to the information already given in

Figure

23
, there are two things to justify about this
figure: the top-left naturality square, and the commutativity of the bottom
square. Let us tackle the former. Recall that Lemma
100

gives us the canonical isomorphism

\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}Y_{r^{\prime}}\quad\cong\quad\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})}Y_{r^{\prime}}\;\otimes\;\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\looparrowleft t^{\prime}(r^{\prime\prime})}Y_{r^{\prime}}

which is natural in
Y_{r^{\prime}}
for
r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
. By instantiating with
Y_{r^{\prime}}=M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}
, we get

\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}\quad\cong\quad\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}\;\otimes\;\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\looparrowleft t^{\prime}(r^{\prime\prime})}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}\quad=\quad N\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}O_{r^{\prime\prime}}

by definition of
N
and
O_{r^{\prime\prime}}
. Hence the vertical canonical isomorphism
at the top left of
Figure

23
; as for the top middle,
it is the instantiation of the same natural isomorphism with
Y_{r^{\prime}}=M
, as
already observed in Definition
E
. To make the top-left
square a naturality square, we should then have

(\ldots)\otimes\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}\psi_{r^{\prime\prime}}\quad=\quad\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\in\partial(t^{\prime})}\widetilde{F}(t)_{r^{\prime}}\;\otimes\;\bigotimes_{r^{\prime\prime}\in R^{\prime\prime}}\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\looparrowleft t^{\prime}(r^{\prime\prime})}\widetilde{F}(t)_{r^{\prime}}

which is indeed the case with our definition of
\psi_{r^{\prime\prime}}
.

Our next and final task is the commutativity of the bottom square of

Figure

23
. Thanks to the bifunctoriality of
\otimes
,
it reduces to a simpler commutative diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 52.19305pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-52.19305pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\displaystyle O_{r^{\prime\prime}}=\bigotimes_{r^{\prime}\looparrowleft t^{\prime}(r^{\prime\prime})}M^{\looparrowleft t(r^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.7778pt\raise-25.777pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.83438pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-28.45273pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.7911pt\raise 8.00891pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.50891pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\widetilde{F}(t^{\prime})_{r^{\prime\prime}}\circ\psi_{r^{\prime\prime}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 137.55132pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 137.55132pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-29.72289pt\raise-38.8217pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\displaystyle M^{\looparrowleft(t\circ
